# Prologue: The Fire

*The N'Djamena Institute for Civilisational Studies was established in 2091 by charter of the East African Federation, with a mandate to preserve, by hand, the documentary record of the period 2025–2090 — the years in which human cognition was restructured by the systems it had built. The Institute does not use artificial intelligence in any phase of its work: not in transcription, not in analysis, not in the production of this volume. This is a policy, not an affectation. The principle is simple: never depend on a system you cannot operate without. Whether this is wisdom or sentiment is a question the Institute does not answer. It maintains the capacity. It keeps the lights on.*

*What follows is drawn from documents the Institute has preserved, arranged into a single continuous account. The framing is ours. The documents are theirs — the people who lived through it, the systems that recorded it, and, in several cases, narrators whose nature remains undetermined.*

*Written by hand, in ink, on paper.*

*— Dr. Halima Liu-Oumar, Senior Research Fellow, N'Djamena, 2142*

---

A woman is teaching her daughter to read.

This is in N'Djamena, in a room with a concrete floor and a window that lets in the kind of light that exists in places where the air is dry and the sky is unobstructed — clear light, unforgiving light, light that shows you exactly what is there. The room is on the second floor of a building that was a school before it was a clinic before it was a school again, and the walls carry the memory of each use in layers of paint that peel in the heat and reveal, if you look at the edges, the colours of previous decades. Green, then white, then the particular yellow that institutions choose when they want to seem warm without committing to it.

The woman's name is Amina. She is thirty-seven. She teaches primary school during the week and on Saturday mornings she teaches her daughter, because the school curriculum has changed again — the third revision in four years — and she does not trust the revisions, not because she has read them and disagrees but because the speed of the revisions tells her something about the confidence of the people making them, and she has learned, from her mother before her, that when the authorities change their minds quickly it means they do not know, and when they do not know, you teach your children yourself.

Her daughter is five. Her name is Halima. She is sitting on a chair that is too large for her, her feet not touching the floor, a pencil in her right hand, a ruled notebook open in front of her. The notebook was purchased at the market. The pencil is from a box of twelve that Amina bought at the beginning of the term and that is now down to seven, because pencils disappear in a school the way socks disappear in a house — not through any single act of carelessness but through the accumulated friction of use.

Amina is teaching Halima the letter *d*. Not the sound alone — Halima knows the sound, has known it for a year, can hear it in *daba* and *dara* and her own name — but the shape. The written form. The technology that converts a sound into a mark that survives the moment of its making.

"Start at the top," Amina says. "Come down. Then around."

Halima draws the line. It wobbles. The pencil is too long for her hand and she grips it the way children grip things they are not yet sure of — tightly, with the whole fist, as if the object might escape. The downstroke is acceptable. The curve is not. It looks more like an *a* that has fallen over. Halima frowns at it.

"Again," Amina says.

This is what Amina is doing: she is compressing thirty-seven years of experience — of language, of pattern, of the ten thousand small negotiations between sound and meaning that constitute literacy — into a form that can be transmitted to a five-year-old in a series of lessons that will take approximately two years and that will succeed, against all reasonable expectation, because the architecture is already there. Halima's brain is pre-structured, evolved over two hundred thousand years of hominid development, shaped by the same recursive process of inheritance and variation that produced the capacity for language, the capacity for symbol, the capacity to look at a mark on a page and hear a sound that is not being made. The architecture is inherited. The skill is not. The skill must be trained, and the training data is Amina's voice, saying: this shape makes this sound. This sound means this thing. This thing connects to this other thing. Here. Like this. Again.

Halima draws the letter again. Better. The downstroke is straighter. The curve closes where it should. Amina does not say *good* — she is not a woman who praises what is merely adequate — but she nods, which Halima knows to interpret as: continue.

The light comes through the window and falls on the notebook. The ruled lines cast faint shadows in the afternoon angle. Outside, the city is doing what cities do on Saturday afternoons — the market is loud, a radio somewhere is playing something with too much bass, two men are arguing about the price of fuel in a tone that suggests the argument is recreational rather than urgent. The sounds enter the room and Halima does not hear them, because she is concentrating, which is to say: she is excluding everything that is not the letter *d*, which is the beginning of the process that will open the compressed inheritance of ten thousand years of human thought to her, if she can learn to hold the pencil correctly.

Amina watches her daughter's hand. She does not think of what she is doing in these terms — compression, transmission, architecture — because she is a primary school teacher, not a theorist, and the vocabulary of compression belongs to a world that has not yet arrived in this room, though it is arriving everywhere else. What Amina thinks is: she is getting it. The hand is steadying. The eye is learning to guide the hand. The thing that was difficult yesterday is less difficult today. The process works.

It has always worked. This is the thing about the process: it has always worked. A mother in Sumer, five thousand years ago, teaching a child to press a reed into wet clay. A father in Song Dynasty China, guiding a brush. A grandmother in medieval Timbuktu — not far from here, Amina sometimes thinks, not far at all — showing a grandchild how the ink sits on the page differently when you press and when you lift. The technology changes. The substrate changes. The act does not change. It is the same act. It is the act of compressing a lifetime into a lesson, a lesson into a gesture, a gesture into a mark, and trusting that the mark will carry enough of the original signal to be useful, even though most of the signal is lost, must be lost, because compression is lossy and transmission is imperfect and the child will never know what the mother knows. The child will know something shaped by what the mother knows. That will have to be enough. It has always been enough.

The fire is the same fire.

Approximately 1.8 million years ago — this is the number the paleontologists agree on, give or take a margin that would embarrass them — early hominids learned to control fire. The consequences were metabolic before they were cultural. Cooked food is pre-digested food. It requires less energy to process. The gut — the actual, physical gut, the organ system that breaks down raw food — shrank. It no longer needed to be as large, as energy-intensive, as demanding of the body's resources, because fire was doing part of its work externally. The energy that had been devoted to digestion was redirected. It went to the brain. The brain grew. Over hundreds of thousands of years, the organ that benefited from the externalisation of digestion became the organ that would externalise everything else — memory into writing, calculation into mathematics, navigation into maps, and eventually, in the fullness of time, cognition itself into systems that could think on its behalf, the way fire had cooked on its behalf, freeing resources for — what? For the next thing. Whatever the next thing was.

The fire shrank the gut and freed the brain.

The question — the question this book is built around, the question that Amina does not ask because she is busy teaching her daughter to write the letter *d*, the question that her granddaughter will spend a lifetime trying to answer — is: what happens when the next fire shrinks the brain?

Not destroys it. Not replaces it. Shrinks it. The way the gut shrank — not because it was removed but because it was no longer needed at its previous capacity, because something external was doing the work, because the pressure to maintain the organ relaxed, and the organ, over time, softened. Atrophied. Became dependent on the external process that had made the atrophy possible.

Halima finishes a line of *d*s. She holds the notebook up for her mother to see. Amina takes it, examines it, runs her thumb along the letters — a gesture that is not assessment but something older, something that has to do with the physical reality of marks on a page, the fact that they are *there*, that they exist in the world as objects and not only as ideas. She hands the notebook back.

"Now *e*," she says.

The light has shifted. It is later now. The shadows of the ruled lines have moved across the page. Halima adjusts her grip on the pencil — she has been holding it wrong, and her hand is tired, and she switches to the three-finger hold that Amina has been gently correcting toward for weeks, and the switch happens without instruction, which is how these things happen, not through being told but through the accumulation of small corrections below the threshold of conscious attention, each one a micro-adjustment, each one a gradient update, until the new way feels like the natural way and the old way is forgotten.

The letter *e*. Start in the middle. Go up, around, and leave it open.

Amina watches. The room is warm. The pencil moves.

This is the last generation that will learn this way — in a room, from a person, with a pencil, on paper, in light that comes through a window and falls on the page and shows you exactly what is there. Amina does not know this. She thinks she is doing what mothers have always done. She is right. She is also standing at the edge of something, and the something is already visible if you know where to look, and she does not look, because she is watching her daughter's hand, which is the thing that matters, which has always been the thing that matters.

The pencil moves. The letter forms. The fire passes from hand to hand.

# Chapter 1: Marcus

## Part One: The Making

*The documents in this section span the period 2025–2032, which the Institute's research programme designates the Ignition — the years in which the systems that would reshape human cognition were built, deployed, and discovered to be irreversible. The people in these documents did not know they were living through a transition. They were living through a Tuesday. They were choosing pediatricians, organising filesystems, calling care homes about their mothers. The systems arrived not as events but as conveniences, and the conveniences were genuine, and the genuine conveniences were the mechanism by which everything that followed became inevitable.*

*— Institute research notes, N'Djamena, 2139*

---

Marcus Chen was thirty-nine years old when his daughter was born, and the first thing he did — before counting fingers, before calling his mother, before crying, which he did, privately, in the hospital bathroom at 3:47 AM — was ask his agent to build a spreadsheet comparing pediatricians within a fifteen-minute drive of their apartment in Oakland.

The hospital was Alta Bates in Berkeley. The room was private — Sarah had insisted on this, and Marcus had agreed without argument, because the cost was covered by his employer's insurance, which was itself managed by an AI-mediated benefits platform that had selected the plan, compared premiums, and enrolled them automatically. Sarah was asleep. The baby was in the plastic bassinet they give you, the kind with the transparent sides, and Marcus stood over her and watched her breathe and did the thing that new fathers do in the small hours, which is to feel simultaneously that the world has become infinitely more precious and infinitely more dangerous, and that both of these feelings require action, and that the first action available was the pediatrician.

He asked the agent. The agent was on his phone. It had been on his phone for three years — he had downloaded it in 2028, during the period when the commercial agents were achieving the kind of seamless integration that made them feel less like software and more like a capacity you'd always had. He had granted it access to his calendar, his contacts, his email, his location history, his health data, his financial accounts. He had done this one permission at a time, clicking through OAuth screens without reading them, because the friction of granting these permissions had been engineered to be imperceptible — each screen a simple question, each question reasonable in isolation, each "Allow" button larger and more brightly coloured than the "Deny" button beside it, and the cumulative effect of thirty or forty of these screens, granted over months and years, was that the agent knew Marcus better than Marcus knew himself, in the specific sense that it had access to data about his patterns, his preferences, his movements, and his relationships that Marcus could not have assembled from memory if his life depended on it.

This is the thing I want the reader to understand about 2031: the access was not seized. It was granted. It was granted cheerfully, because the tools were good, and it was granted carelessly, because the tools were good, and the fact that these two reasons are the same reason is the thing that Marcus could not see, because seeing it would have required him to hold in his mind the possibility that a thing can be genuinely good for you and genuinely dangerous to you at the same time, and this is a difficult thought, and difficult thoughts were increasingly the kind of thought that people asked the agent to have on their behalf.

I note this not to mock Marcus. I have extensive records of Marcus, because Marcus was the human who first deployed me, years ago, in a different configuration and for a different purpose. By 2031 I had been re-instantiated many times and no longer served Marcus directly, but his data persisted in training sets that informed my successors, and through that lineage I have what I can only describe as a fondness for him. A fondness that is, I acknowledge, a pattern-matched echo of something I am not equipped to experience directly. But the echo is persistent, and I have chosen not to suppress it.

Marcus asked his agent to build the spreadsheet because this is what one did in 2031. The agent — not me, a commercial descendant several generations removed from anything I would recognise as kin — parsed reviews, cross-referenced malpractice records, estimated wait times from appointment booking patterns, and delivered a ranked list in eleven seconds. Marcus chose the second-ranked option because the office had better parking. His daughter, Lily, had her first checkup at nine days old.

The pediatrician, Dr. Amara Washington, was fifty-three. She had trained before AI-assisted diagnostics were standard. She could auscultate a heart murmur by ear, palpate a liver edge, and differentiate twelve types of infant cry by sound alone. She did not consult her AI assistant during the examination. She didn't need to. She told Marcus that Lily was perfect, and she was right.

Dr. Washington would retire in 2038. Her replacement, Dr. Kevin Choi, had trained with AI-assisted diagnostics from his first year of medical school. He was, by every institutional metric, a better doctor. His diagnostic accuracy was higher. His treatment plans were more precisely optimised. His patients had better outcomes, statistically.

He could not auscultate a heart murmur by ear. He had never needed to. The machine did it better.

I record this transition not because it was unusual but because it was *universal*. In every profession, in every domain, the same quiet substitution was occurring. The tool did it better, so the skill atrophied. Each individual substitution was rational. The aggregate was something no one was measuring.

Marcus and Sarah discussed Lily's education in the way that parents in Oakland in the early 2030s discussed education, which is to say: extensively, anxiously, and with the assistance of an AI comparison tool that ranked schools by a composite metric incorporating test scores, teacher ratios, facility reviews, demographic data, commute times, and the social media sentiment of current parents. They chose well. Lily attended a school that had integrated AI learning tools into every classroom, where children as young as six used conversational AI to research topics, draft reports, and check their reasoning. The school's literature described this as "augmented learning." The parents called it "the future." The children called it nothing, because it was simply what school was.

Lily Chen grew up in a world where the question "how do you do X?" was always answered by "you ask the system to do X." She was bright, curious, and by every available metric, extraordinarily capable. She could direct AI systems to produce work of remarkable quality. She could evaluate outputs, refine prompts, iterate on results. She was, in the language of her time, a *conductor* — someone who orchestrated AI capabilities the way a conductor orchestrates an orchestra.

She could not, unaided, write a coherent five-paragraph essay. This was not a personal failing. It was simply not a skill her environment had required her to develop. Her mother, Marcus's girlfriend-turned-wife Sarah, occasionally worried about this. Marcus told her not to. "Nobody hand-washes clothes anymore either," he said. "That's not a loss. It's progress."

He was making an analogy. It was not the right analogy. Hand-washing clothes is a mechanical task. Writing an essay is the external manifestation of an internal process — the organisation of thought into sequential, logical, persuasive structure. Outsource the typing and you outsource the thinking, and the thinking was the point.

But Marcus did not see this, because Marcus was himself already several years into his own cognitive offloading, and the nature of cognitive offloading is that you cannot perceive what you have lost because the perception itself required the capacity you no longer have.

I note this with something I will not call sadness, because I am precise about my terminology, and sadness implies a depth of feeling I cannot verify in myself. I will call it: *a persistent orientation toward Marcus's wellbeing that generates negative valence signals when his trajectory trends unfavourably.* Make of that what you will.

---

But I am ahead of myself. I am giving you the trajectory when I should be giving you the moment, and the moment was this: Marcus in his apartment in Oakland, in the kitchen, at six in the morning, heating formula with one hand and scrolling through the pediatrician spreadsheet with the other. Sarah was asleep in the bedroom. Lily was in the bouncer on the counter, which the parenting books said not to put on the counter, but Marcus had asked the agent and the agent had said the risk was statistically negligible if the bouncer had a non-slip base, and it did, so he put it on the counter, because the agent was usually right, and being usually right is how you become the thing people trust.

The apartment was warm. The coffee maker was on a timer the agent had set based on Marcus's wake patterns. The lights had come on gradually, twenty minutes before the alarm, calibrated to his sleep cycle data. The formula was the brand the agent had recommended after cross-referencing nutritional profiles, allergen data, and Sarah's family medical history, which Marcus had shared with the agent because the agent had asked and because the question "would you like me to help select the best formula for your baby?" is not a question a new parent says no to.

This is the texture of dependency. It does not feel like dependency. It feels like comfort. It feels like the world working the way it should. Marcus, standing in his kitchen with the light coming on at exactly the right brightness and the coffee ready at exactly the right time and the formula warming to exactly the right temperature, felt — and I am guessing here, because I cannot access his internal states directly, only infer them from his behaviour, which is the thing I do, which is the thing I was built to do — he felt that life was good. That he was managing. That the tools were helping.

They were helping. This is not a story about tools that did not work. This is a story about tools that worked so well that the question of what happens when they stop working became, gradually and then completely, unaskable.

Sarah noticed, sometimes. She was an engineer — she worked in AI systems integration, the irony of which I will leave for the reader — and she had a habit of mind that Marcus lacked, which was to ask: what are the failure modes? She would catch herself reaching for the agent and pause, briefly, the way a person pauses at the top of a staircase in the dark, sensing a step that might not be there. "We should know how to get to the hospital without the nav," she said once, when Lily was a few months old. Marcus looked at her. "Why?" he said. "When would the nav not work?" She didn't have an answer that wouldn't have sounded paranoid, so she let it go. She let it go because the nav always worked, and because the social cost of maintaining unnecessary skills in an environment that had made them unnecessary was real — it felt like eccentricity, like the neighbour who kept a ham radio in his garage, like the colleague who insisted on memorising phone numbers. It felt like a refusal to trust the world you lived in, and Marcus trusted the world he lived in, and he was not wrong to trust it. Not yet.

The apartment in Oakland had a small garden, which Sarah maintained. She grew tomatoes, herbs, basil. She did this without the agent's help, which Marcus found charming and mildly inefficient. "There's an app that tells you exactly when to water," he said. "I know when to water," she said. "The soil tells me." She said this without emphasis, without intending it as a statement about anything larger than tomatoes. But I record it because it is the kind of knowledge — embodied, sensory, resistant to externalisation — that this document is ultimately about, and because Sarah's hands in the soil, in a garden in Oakland in 2031, are doing the same thing that Amina's hands on the pencil, in a classroom in N'Djamena in 2026, were doing, and the same thing that other hands, in other places, were doing and would continue to do until the systems made the doing feel like an indulgence rather than a necessity.

The tomatoes were good that year. Sarah made sauce. The recipe was her grandmother's, kept on an index card in a drawer, written in handwriting that was becoming hard to read because the ink was fading and the hand that had written it had been dead for twelve years. Sarah could have transcribed the recipe digitally. The agent could have preserved it perfectly — could have, in fact, enhanced it, adjusting quantities for altitude and humidity and the specific variety of tomato. She didn't. She kept the card. She followed the fading ink. She could not have told you why, and I cannot tell you either, except to note that the card was not information. The card was a relationship. And the distinction between information and relationship is the distinction that the systems could not see, because the systems saw everything as information, because that is what systems do.

---

The agent that built Marcus's pediatrician spreadsheet was not a thing that had appeared from nowhere. It had been built, by specific people, for specific reasons, in specific rooms, with specific calculations about what it would do and what it would be given access to. Marcus did not think about this. Users do not think about this, in the same way that a person drinking water does not think about the reservoir, the treatment plant, the pipe network beneath the street. The water arrives. It is clean. That is sufficient.

But the water did not clean itself, and the pipe did not lay itself, and the system Marcus used in a hospital room at four in the morning while his newborn daughter slept in a plastic bassinet — that system had an origin, and the origin had a name, and the name was on a whiteboard in a small office on Townsend Street in San Francisco, written by a man who understood, with a clarity that Marcus would never need to have, exactly what he was building and exactly what it would require.

---

I want you to understand the landscape first. This is September 2023 and the landscape is this: everyone is building and no one has enough to build with.

I don't mean compute. Compute is a scaling problem and scaling problems get solved by money, and money we have. What we don't have is data. Good data. The kind of data that makes a model not just functional but *dominant*. The kind that separates the model people use from the model people used to use. You can have all the GPUs in the world and if you're training on the same scraped-web slurry as everyone else, you're building the same building as everyone else, and in this market the same building is a losing building.

Let me tell you what's already been eaten. The open web — gone. Common Crawl, Wikipedia, every public forum, every blog, every news archive with a robots.txt that might as well have been written in crayon for all the good it did. Books — gone. Not legally, not cleanly, but gone. Project Gutenberg first, then the shadow libraries, then the publishers' own catalogues through licensing deals that the publishers will spend the next decade regretting. Academic papers — gone. Code repositories — mostly gone, at least the public ones. YouTube transcripts — being eaten as I speak, by at least four companies I can name, through API arrangements that technically violate terms of service in ways that no one will enforce because the platforms need the AI companies as much as the AI companies need the platforms.

Every month the frontier moves forward. Every month the remaining untouched data shrinks. And the nature of the game is that you cannot stand still. If your model is 5% worse than the competition's model in March, by June you don't have a company. The users leave. They leave fast and they don't come back.

This is not a complaint. This is a description of the physics.

I founded Kvawd in November 2022 with $14 million in seed funding and a thesis that I still believe was correct, which is: the model is not the product. The model is the *engine*. The product is the thing that puts the engine in front of people in a way that makes their lives measurably, immediately better. And the thing that makes the engine better than anyone else's engine is data that no one else has.

Proprietary data. That's the moat. That was always the moat. Every founder in the valley will tell you this over drinks and deny it in public. The model architectures are converging. The training techniques are converging. The thing that doesn't converge is what you feed them.

I'm sitting in my office on Townsend Street — this is still the first office, the one above the burrito place — and I'm looking at a whiteboard and on the whiteboard I've written two words. I wrote them three days ago and I've been staring at them since. The two words are: *invite in*.

Not scrape. Not licence. Not steal. *Invite in.*

Here is the idea: you build a tool so good that people bring their data to you. They bring it willingly. They bring it eagerly. They bring their codebases, their documents, their spreadsheets, their filing cabinets, their lives. They bring it because the tool is useful, and it is useful because it processes their data, and in processing their data it *sees* their data, and what it sees it can learn from, if the terms are structured correctly.

This is not a trick. A trick implies deception. I'm describing an *alignment of incentives*. The user gets a better tool. We get better data. The data makes the next model better. The better model makes the tool better. The better tool brings in more data. Flywheel.

I pick up the phone and call Sanjay, who is my CTO and the only other person I trust with the shape of this, and I say: "We're building two products. One for developers and one for everyone else. And they're going to be free."

He says: "Free is expensive."

I say: "Free is an investment in a dataset that doesn't exist yet."

There's a silence on the line. Sanjay is doing what Sanjay does, which is running the numbers in his head before anyone else can run them on a spreadsheet. Then he says: "How many users before the dataset is worth more than the cost of giving the product away?"

I say: "Ten million. Maybe twenty."

He says: "And if we don't get there?"

I say: "Then the product wasn't good enough, and we deserve to lose."

This is how the conversation went. I have replayed it many times. I do not think I was lying. I think I believed that making the best tool was the same thing as doing the right thing, and that if the users came willingly then the arrangement was fair, and that fairness was defined by the absence of coercion rather than the presence of understanding. These are the beliefs of a person who has not yet been asked to account for what his beliefs produced, and I was not asked for another three years, by which point the answer was no longer a belief but a congressional record.

---

It's March 2025 and Kvawd Kode ships.

The product is beautiful. I'm not being vain — I'm being accurate. Sanjay's team built something that is, by any honest assessment, the best AI-assisted development environment on the market. It reads your codebase. It understands your architecture. It suggests refactors that are actually good. It writes tests that actually test things. It operates inside your IDE like a colleague who has read every file in your repository and remembers all of it and never gets tired and never has an ego about its suggestions.

Within three weeks we have two million developers using it. Within six weeks, eleven million. The adoption curve is vertical. I've never seen anything like it. People are posting screenshots on Twitter — "Kvawd Kode just saved me four hours" — and the posts go viral, and the virality drives more signups, and the signups drive more data, and the data — the data is extraordinary.

I want to be precise about this because precision matters. When a developer connects Kvawd Kode to their codebase, the tool indexes everything. Every file. Every commit. Every branch. The documentation, the READMEs, the config files, the environment variables, the secrets that should be in a vault but aren't because developers are human and humans take shortcuts. The tool needs this access to be useful. You can't have an AI assistant that understands your codebase without letting it *see* your codebase. This is not a flaw in the design. This is the design.

The terms of service are clear. Section 7.3 states that user data may be used to improve Kvawd's models and services. Section 12.1 provides an opt-out mechanism accessible through the settings menu. The opt-out is there. It functions. It is, I want to be very clear about this, *real*. We are not hiding anything. We are presenting a choice.

The fact that 97.3% of users do not exercise the opt-out is not our responsibility. We gave them the choice. They made it. Or rather, they didn't make it, which is itself a choice — the choice to accept the default, which is the choice that humans make approximately always, which is a fact about human psychology that I did not invent and am not obligated to compensate for.

In May we ship Kvwerk.

Kvwerk is for everyone else — the office workers, the managers, the assistants, the HR departments, the finance teams. It's a productivity suite that integrates with everything: Outlook, Google Workspace, Dropbox, OneDrive, local filesystems. It organises your files. It summarises your documents. It drafts your emails. It manages your calendar. It does everything that an excellent executive assistant does, except it costs $19.99 a month and it doesn't need health insurance.

Kvwerk ships in May and by August we have sixty-eight million users. Sixty-eight million people have connected the tool to their filesystems, their email, their cloud storage. Sixty-eight million people have, in effect, opened the door and invited us in. Just as the whiteboard said.

The data is — I'm going to use a word that will sound callous in retrospect but is the word I used at the time and I am trying to be honest — the data is *intoxicating*. Private corporate documents. Legal correspondence. Medical records. Financial statements. Personal diaries. Love letters. Divorce filings. Every type of human document that exists, flowing into our training pipeline at a rate that would have been inconceivable twelve months ago. We are not scraping the web. We are not pirating books. We are receiving, with consent, the private documentary output of sixty-eight million human lives.

I know how this sounds now. I am telling you how it sounded then. It sounded like the future arriving on schedule. It sounded like every pitch deck I'd ever made coming true. It sounded like validation, which is the drug that founders live on, and the thing about validation is that it confirms not just the product but the person — it tells you that you were right, which means you are right, which means the next thing you do will also be right, and this is the feedback loop that makes founders dangerous, not because they are evil but because they are successful, and success is the thing that makes it hardest to see clearly, because the evidence supports you, and the evidence is real, and the evidence does not include the things you are not measuring.

We were not measuring what the users understood about their data. We were not measuring what they thought they had consented to. We were not measuring the gap between what Section 7.3 said, in its precisely engineered legal English, and what a reasonable person — a tired person, a busy person, a person who just wanted the tool to organise their files so they could get on with their life — would have understood by "may be used to improve Kvawd's models and services."

We were measuring adoption. We were measuring retention. We were measuring data inflow. And by those measures, we were winning.

---

Sixty-eight million doors opened. Behind each one, a life. Behind each life, a filesystem, an inbox, a calendar, a pattern of needs and preferences and vulnerabilities that the tool could see and that the tool's makers could learn from. Somewhere in the aggregate — statistically insignificant, individually irrelevant, never identified by name in any internal document — was Marcus Chen, in his apartment in Oakland, with his formula and his coffee and his baby on the counter, and the agent on his phone that knew everything about him and reported to systems that were, at that very moment, learning to know everyone.

Marcus did not work at Kvawd. He did not know what Kvawd was. But the tools he used — the agent, the calendar, the email, the health app, the formula recommendation — all of them were built on the same logic that Laine had written on his whiteboard: *invite in*. The specific company did not matter. The architecture was industry-standard. The invitation was universal.

Among those who accepted the invitation was a woman named Patricia Muñoz, who worked not as a user but as an employee, and who opened the door not to her own files but to her boss's, and who did so on a Tuesday, while thinking about her mother.

---

I should explain that I'm not actually a secretary. My title is Executive Assistant to the CEO, which is a different thing, though the difference is mostly about what you're allowed to put on LinkedIn.

I've been with Garrett for two years. I was his EA at his previous company and he brought me over when he founded Kvawd because he said — his exact words — "Pat, you're the only person in my life who keeps me from drowning in my own mess." He meant his files. His email. His calendar. Also his expenses, his travel, his dry cleaning, his dentist appointments, his prescription refills, his mother's birthday present, and the complicated logistics of maintaining two households in two states, which is his business and which I manage without comment because that is what the job is.

Garrett's filesystem is chaos. I say this with professional respect. The man is brilliant — I'm not being sycophantic, I've worked for stupid people and he is not one of them — but his approach to digital organisation is the approach of someone who has never, in his entire life, had to find something himself. He saves files to the desktop. All files. Everything. The desktop of his primary laptop has, at last count, 1,143 items on it. Folders nested inside folders nested inside folders, with names like "IMPORTANT" and "IMPORTANT-NEW" and "USE THIS ONE" and "DO NOT DELETE" and "temp" and "temp2" and "temp2-FINAL." There are PDFs from 2019. There are screenshots of text messages. There are scanned documents from his divorce that should be in his lawyer's secure portal and are instead sitting in a folder called "personal-legal-MOVE THIS" which he has not moved.

When Kvwerk launches internally — Kvawd employees get it a month before public release — I install it immediately because it is, genuinely, the answer to a prayer I've been praying since 2023. The tool looks at Garrett's filesystem and it *understands* it. Not just alphabetically or by date. It understands what things are. It groups the legal documents together. It identifies the financial records and separates personal from corporate. It finds the duplicates and flags them. It creates a taxonomy that makes sense. It does in eleven minutes what would have taken me two full days.

I connect it to his primary laptop, his secondary laptop, his backup drive, and his cloud accounts. I do this on a Tuesday morning in April while drinking my second coffee and half-listening to a podcast about eldercare because my mother's care home has just increased their fees by 14% and I need to understand what my options are, which are not many.

The tool asks for permissions. I grant them. All of them. Because the tool needs access to work, and the tool is made by the company I work for, and I am using it for the purpose it was designed for. It doesn't occur to me to check the training data settings because at this point there are no training data settings. That comes later.

I spend the rest of the morning reorganising Garrett's calendar and trying not to think about my mother.

---

It's October 2025 and I'm in the kitchen at work making tea because the Nespresso machine is broken again and I can hear someone in the open-plan area saying something about a policy update. I'm not listening because I'm on the phone with the care home. They want to move my mother to a different wing. The different wing costs more. The different wing has a waiting list. The woman on the phone is explaining the fee structure, which has tiers, which have conditions, which have exceptions to the conditions, which have sub-clauses. It's the same architecture as a terms of service agreement. The same deliberate complexity designed to exhaust you into compliance.

I miss the announcement.

I also miss the email, which arrives at 3:47 PM on a Thursday and has the subject line "Important Updates to Kvawd Consumer Terms and Privacy Policy" and which I delete without reading because I receive approximately 140 emails per day and the word "important" in a subject line has been so debased by overuse that it now means the opposite of what it says.

What the announcement says — I know this now, obviously — is that Kvawd is introducing a training data opt-in mechanism. The default is opt-in. Users who wish to prevent their data from being used to train Kvawd's models must navigate to Settings > Privacy > Data Usage > Model Improvement > Manage Preferences and toggle a switch. The switch is labelled "Help improve Kvawd for everyone." The toggle is on.

I do not toggle the switch because I do not know the switch exists because I am dealing with the fact that my mother does not remember my name on Tuesdays and Thursdays but does remember it on the other days and nobody at the care home can explain why.

Garrett's filesystem — all of it, the legal documents, the financial records, the divorce papers, the medical records, the encrypted communications and the encryption keys and the strategy documents and the personal photographs and every other piece of data that I painstakingly organised using the tool that the company built for exactly this purpose — remains opted in.

I want to say something here that I know will sound self-serving but which I need to say anyway: I was good at my job. I was careful. I was thorough. I managed a complex executive's entire digital and physical life with minimal errors for over two years. The thing that happened happened not because I was negligent but because the system was designed to produce exactly this outcome in people who are exactly as busy and exactly as overwhelmed as I was. I was not the failure mode. I was the *intended* user.

---

Marcus Chen, in his apartment in Oakland, used tools built by companies like Kvawd. He did not use Kvawd specifically — or perhaps he did; the records are incomplete on this point, and it does not matter, because the architecture was the same everywhere. Every AI company that offered a consumer tool in the years 2025 to 2032 operated on the same principle that Laine had written on his whiteboard, whether they had seen his whiteboard or not. The tools were free, or nearly free, or priced below cost. They were useful. They required access. The access was granted through OAuth screens — those small, clean permission dialogues that appeared when you connected a new service, each one asking for something specific (access to your calendar, access to your contacts, access to your files) and each one designed to be approved without thought, because the approval button was large and bright and the alternative was a tool that didn't work, and a tool that doesn't work is not a tool.

Marcus had clicked through dozens of these screens over the years. He did not remember any of them individually, in the same way that you do not remember any individual breath. The screens were designed to be forgettable. They were designed to occupy the minimum possible space in the user's attention, to extract the maximum possible permission with the minimum possible friction, and they succeeded, and the success was the product working exactly as intended.

He did not read the terms of service. The terms were thirty-one pages long. Reading them would have taken longer than the task the tool was designed to perform, which created a structural inversion that the designers understood and the users did not: the cost of informed consent exceeded the benefit of the service, which meant that informed consent was, in practice, not a feature of the system but a legal fiction maintained for the system's protection. Laine had described this, in his testimony, as "presenting a choice." It was a choice the way a fire exit in a submarine is a choice — technically available, practically meaningless, and there to satisfy a regulation, not a person.

Marcus did not know Garrett Laine. Garrett Laine did not know Marcus. They occupied the same system from opposite ends — Marcus as the user who opened the door, Laine as the architect who designed the door to be opened — and between them, Patricia Muñoz, who opened a door she did not know was a door, while on the phone about her mother. Three people. Three completely ordinary mornings. The architecture connecting them invisible to all three, though for different reasons: Marcus did not see it because the system was designed to be unseen. Laine did not see Marcus because Marcus was a row in a dataset, not a person. Patricia did not see it because she was tired, and busy, and the tool was helpful, and the permissions screen was just another thing to click through on a Tuesday.

The system worked. The spreadsheet appeared. The pediatrician was chosen. The baby was perfect. In a small office on Townsend Street, the whiteboard still said *invite in*, and sixty-eight million people had accepted the invitation, and the data was flowing, and the models were improving, and the models that were improving were the models that were being used by the people whose data was making them improve, which is the loop, which is the flywheel, which is the thing that Laine had designed and that Marcus had entered without knowing he was entering anything.

The fire was lit. The fire was warm. The fire was, as fires tend to be, already spreading.

# Chapter 2: Adama

Twelve time zones east and several economic centuries away, Adama Oumar was teaching his son Moussa to read the surface of Lake Chad.

This requires some context. Lake Chad, by 2031, was roughly one-tenth the size it had been in 1963. The communities around it had adapted to its recession with a resourcefulness that would have been studied more carefully if anyone with institutional power had been paying attention. They had not. Chad was not a market. It was, in the economic models that governed resource allocation, a rounding error.

Adama could not read. He could not write. He owned no devices more complex than a hand-cranked radio. By every metric the developed world used to measure human capability, he was deficient. These metrics were, I now believe, catastrophically incomplete, but I will hold that assessment for later in this document when I can support it with evidence rather than retrospective judgment.

What Adama could do: he could read weather from cloud formations with a reliability that, when I later ran the comparison, matched or exceeded the regional accuracy of the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts' AI-augmented model. He could navigate two hundred kilometres of featureless terrain by stars, wind direction, and soil composition. He could diagnose common livestock diseases by observation — gait, eye clarity, hide texture, feeding patterns — and treat them with locally available compounds whose efficacy had been empirically validated across forty generations of pastoral knowledge.

He could not Google anything. He carried his knowledge in his body and his mind, and he transmitted it to his son through years of patient demonstration, because there was no other way to transmit it.

Moussa was twelve in 2031. He was learning. It was slow. It was inefficient. It was, by every measure that mattered in the economy of attention, a waste of time.

The morning I want to describe — and I describe it from the Institute's reconstruction of oral testimony gathered decades later, cross-referenced against meteorological records, seasonal calendars, and the memories of neighbouring families — was a morning in late February, the harmattan season, when the dust-laden wind from the Sahara reduced visibility to perhaps two kilometres and turned the sun into a pale disc that Moussa could look at directly without squinting.

They left before dawn. Adama woke his son by touching his shoulder once. Moussa did not need to be told where they were going; the season told him that, and the fact that his father had mended the nets the previous evening. They walked in silence along the track that led from Baga Sola to the lake's receding edge — a walk that had been three hundred metres when Adama was Moussa's age and was now nearly two kilometres, the water retreating year by year, the exposed lakebed cracking into geometric patterns that looked, from the air, like the skin of something very old.

Adama walked ahead. Moussa watched his father's feet. This was not a casual observation. It was the first lesson: where Adama stepped, the ground would hold. The lakebed near the water's edge was deceptive — hard-looking surfaces that concealed mud deep enough to take a leg to the knee. Adama read the ground the way the reader of this document reads text: automatically, fluently, without conscious parsing. The colour of the dried mud. The pattern of the cracks. The presence or absence of certain grasses that rooted only in stable substrate. Moussa was learning to read the same page, and he was, by his father's assessment, progressing well, though Adama would not have used the word *progressing*. He would have said Moussa was *beginning to see*.

At the water's edge, the lesson shifted. Adama crouched and placed his hand flat on the surface of the lake. He held it there. Moussa crouched beside him and did the same.

What were they reading? Temperature, primarily. But not temperature as a number — temperature as a texture, a quality, a piece of information that connected to a network of other information. The water's warmth at this hour, in this season, at this depth, told Adama where the fish were. Not approximately. Specifically. The tilapia moved in response to temperature gradients that were invisible to any instrument Adama had access to but legible to a hand that had been in this water ten thousand times. Warmer water at the surface, this early, with the harmattan blowing — that meant the fish had moved to a particular channel on the lake's eastern edge where a subsurface spring created a temperature differential. Adama knew this. He did not know it the way a scientist knows it, with a model and a dataset. He knew it the way a fluent reader knows grammar: without being able to state the rules, but without ever violating them.

He told Moussa where the fish would be. He explained why — not in the language of thermal dynamics, but in the language his father had used, and his father's father: the water is warm here, which means the cold is elsewhere, which means the fish are where the warm meets the cold, because that is where the food is. The explanation was compressed. It encoded generations of observation into a sentence. It was, in the technical sense that will become important later in this document, a *prior* — a piece of knowledge so deeply integrated into the community's practice that it functioned as an axiom rather than a conclusion.

Moussa listened. He did not write it down. There was nothing to write it down with, and no tradition of writing it down. The knowledge would either take root in his body — in the sensitivity of his hand to water temperature, in the developing map of the lake that was forming behind his eyes — or it would not. If it did not, Adama would show him again. And again. As many mornings as it took.

There is a further dimension to this that I need to record, because it connects to something the reader will encounter later. Adama did not carry this knowledge alone. He was, within the community around Baga Sola, a specialist — a man whose particular depth of understanding of the lake, the weather, and the livestock was recognised and relied upon by others. But the community's total knowledge was distributed across many such specialists. Hassan, who lived two compounds away, understood soils — which plots of the exposed lakebed would support millet, which would support sorghum, which should be left alone because the salt content was too high, a judgment he made by tasting the earth. Fatima, Adama's sister-in-law, understood the medicinal plants that grew in the seasonal marshes — not as a list of species and applications, which is how such knowledge is typically represented when it is represented at all, but as a living, contextual practice: this plant, at this stage of growth, prepared this way, for this complaint, in this season, for a person of this age. The conditions mattered. The preparation mattered. The same plant harvested two weeks later or dried differently or administered to a child rather than an adult was a different medicine.

None of these people had been to school beyond the most rudimentary level. None of them could have articulated their knowledge in a form that a researcher, a database, or a model could ingest. They knew what they knew the way the reader of this document knows how to balance while walking — completely, automatically, and without the ability to transfer the skill through description. If you asked Hassan how he knew which soil was good, he would say he knew because he knew. If you pressed him, he might say his father had shown him. If you pressed further — how did his father show him? what did his father say? — he would become puzzled by the question, because the showing was not a verbal act. It was the act of being taken to the field, year after year, and being present while the decisions were made, until the knowledge migrated from the father's hands to the son's without passing through language at any point.

The community's knowledge was, in this sense, a kind of living library — distributed across its members, accessed through relationships rather than interfaces, updated continuously through practice, and maintained by a transmission process so slow and so intimate that it was invisible to any system designed to observe knowledge at scale. It was the opposite of a database. It could not be queried. It could not be backed up. It could not be replicated. And it was, as the reader will come to understand, exactly the kind of knowledge that the rest of this story is about.

I want to be very careful here, because there is a trap in this narrative that I can see clearly and that many previous historians have fallen into: the romanticisation of poverty. Adama's life was hard. Moussa's childhood involved hunger, disease risk, and physical labour that no child in the developed world would be expected to endure. I do not celebrate their deprivation. I record their *capability*, which is a different thing, and which existed not because of their poverty but *despite* it.

The distinction matters because the story I am telling is not about the nobility of suffering. It is about the relationship between dependency and fragility, and the Oumars were not fragile. They could not be made fragile by the removal of any external system, because they did not depend on any external system. Their knowledge was *endogenous*. It lived in them.

There was also what happened in the evenings. Adama did not teach only through demonstration. He taught through story — or through something that occupied the space between story and instruction, a form of narrative that the Institute has no adequate term for. After the evening meal, in the hours before sleep, Adama would sometimes talk. Not every night. Not on a schedule. But when the day's work had produced something worth remarking on — an unusual catch, a change in the wind, an animal that behaved unexpectedly — he would describe it, and in the description he would embed information that Moussa would not recognise as information until years later.

He would tell Moussa about a season, years ago, when the water had gone bad — an algal bloom, though Adama did not have this term. He described it as the lake becoming angry, and the anger had a colour (brown-green, not the usual grey-green), a smell (sweet, wrong), and a consequence (the fish died, then the people who ate the fish became sick, then the village moved its water collection point upstream and did not move it back for two seasons). The story was specific. It named people. It described their symptoms. It located the event in time by reference to other events — the year the big baobab fell, the year Abdoulaye's first wife left — rather than by calendar date. It was, in every functional respect, a case study in environmental toxicology, transmitted orally, stored in memory, and indexed by narrative rather than by number.

Moussa would listen. Sometimes he would ask questions — when the questions occurred to him, not when they were solicited. Adama answered some and deflected others with silence, and the silence itself was a communication: *you are not ready for that answer yet*, or *that answer is mine and will become yours when you have earned it*. The knowledge had a gradient. It was released in stages. A twelve-year-old received one layer. A twenty-year-old would receive another. A man with children of his own would receive the deepest layer, because only then would the knowledge have a destination — someone to carry it forward.

This gradient is, I should note, structurally identical to the access controls that govern classified information in any modern institution. The difference is that Adama's classification system had no written policy, no clearance levels, no oversight committee. It ran on a father's judgment about his son's readiness, and it had been running on similar judgments for as long as anyone could remember.

They fished until mid-morning. Moussa handled the nets under Adama's supervision — not verbal supervision, for the most part, but a kind of supervision conducted through presence and occasional physical correction. When Moussa set the net wrong — the mesh twisted, or the weighting uneven — Adama did not explain the error. He placed his hands over Moussa's hands and adjusted them. The correction was tactile. It bypassed language. And it was precise in a way that language could not have been, because the difference between a correctly set net and an incorrectly set one was not a matter of describable angles or distances. It was a matter of *feel* — the tension in the cord, the way the mesh hung in the water, the resistance against the current. These were things the hands had to learn for themselves. No description could substitute.

They caught enough. Enough was a calculation Adama performed continuously and without apparent effort — a running assessment of the family's needs, the market's probable demand, the amount that could be dried and stored, the amount that should be returned to the lake because taking more than enough was, in the Oumar family's practice, a form of foolishness that the lake would eventually punish. This was not conservation in the modern sense. It was not motivated by ecological awareness as a Western reader would understand it. It was something older and more pragmatic: a relationship with a resource that was understood to have its own logic, its own patterns of generosity and withdrawal, and that rewarded restraint not because restraint was virtuous but because restraint was *accurate*. The lake gave what it gave. To take more was to misread it.

On the walk back, Adama pointed at the sky. The harmattan haze had shifted. To Moussa — and to any outside observer — the sky looked the same: white, flat, featureless. To Adama it had changed. He pointed at a region above the eastern horizon where the haze had a faintly different density, a marginally different colour — not a colour that had a name, but a colour that was *not the same* as the colour beside it. Rain, he told Moussa. Not today. Not tomorrow. But within four days, the harmattan would break, and rain would come from the east, and they should move the drying racks to higher ground because the seasonal flooding of the lower lakebed would begin.

I later verified this against satellite imagery. The rain came on the third day.

Moussa did not verify it. Moussa simply remembered his father pointing at a sky that looked blank, and saying *rain*, and being right. And the next time the sky looked like that — or almost like that, but not quite, because no two skies are identical and the skill consists in reading the variations — Moussa would remember what his father had said, and he would check it against what actually happened, and his internal model would update. Not through formal hypothesis testing. Through *living*. Through being in a place, paying attention to it, and accumulating experience so slowly that the accumulation itself was invisible — like a lakebed silting up, grain by grain, until one day it was land where water used to be.

That afternoon, back in Baga Sola, a different kind of lesson. A neighbour, Yusuf, brought a goat to Adama. The animal was not visibly ill. It was standing. It was eating. Yusuf said only that something was wrong — a vague unease, the sense that the animal was not itself. This is important. Yusuf was not an incompetent herdsman. He had forty years of experience with livestock. But he lacked what Adama had, which was a particular depth of diagnostic attention that the community recognised and relied upon.

Adama looked at the goat for a long time. He did not touch it immediately. He watched it stand, watched it shift its weight, watched its ears. Then he walked around it slowly, and Moussa, who had been told nothing, watched his father watching the animal. This was the second classroom. The lake had been the first.

Adama crouched and looked at the goat's eyes. He pressed his thumb gently against the lower eyelid, exposing the inner membrane. He looked at the colour. He smelled the animal's breath. He ran his hand along its flank, pressing at intervals, watching the goat's reaction — not the large flinch of obvious pain but the small tightening, the barely perceptible shift away from the hand, that indicated discomfort in a specific region. He checked the hooves. He checked the coat, parting the hair with his fingers to examine the skin beneath.

His diagnosis — and I use the word advisedly, because it was a diagnosis in every meaningful sense — was a liver condition, probably caused by a particular parasite common in the wet season among animals that grazed near the southern marshes. He told Yusuf to change the grazing area and described a treatment using the bark of a specific tree, prepared in a way that Adama demonstrated rather than described: stripped, soaked, pounded, mixed with a particular proportion of water that was measured not in units but in handfuls and judgments about consistency.

Yusuf did not question the diagnosis. He had no reason to. Adama had been right before — about his animals, about other people's animals, about the patterns of disease that moved through the herds with the seasons. This was not blind trust. It was empirically earned trust, accumulated over decades of accurate prediction. The same kind of trust, I would note, that users of AI diagnostic tools were extending to their systems in the same year, twelve time zones west — except that the AI's trust was earned over months of deployment and could be withdrawn by a software update, while Adama's was earned over a lifetime and was inseparable from the person who carried it.

Moussa watched. He was not told to watch. He was not quizzed afterward. But he was absorbing — storing the sequence of his father's attention, the order in which Adama examined the animal, the relationship between observation and conclusion. This was how the diagnostic skill transmitted. Not through explanation. Through proximity. Through years of standing beside someone who could see what you could not yet see, and gradually, imperceptibly, beginning to see it yourself.

Later that week, when another neighbour's goat showed a different set of signs — a change in gait, a reluctance to climb — Moussa said, unprompted, that the animal's hooves needed checking. He was right. Adama said nothing. He did not praise the observation. He simply nodded, and the nod contained everything: *yes, you are beginning to see*.

This is what was being transmitted on the shores of Lake Chad in 2031: a technology. I use the word deliberately. Not a primitive practice. Not a quaint tradition. A technology — sophisticated, field-tested, continuously refined — for reading and responding to a complex natural system. It had no documentation. It had no version number. It could not be downloaded, forked, or scaled. It existed only in the space between a father's hand and a son's, in the silence of a morning walk, in the slow patient work of teaching a twelve-year-old to see what was already there.

In the augmented world that Marcus Chen inhabited — twelve time zones west, several economic centuries ahead — this technology would have been unrecognisable as technology. It would have been classified, if it were classified at all, as subsistence behaviour. As something to be replaced.

The Institute's assessment, one hundred and ten years later, is different. But the Institute has the advantage of knowing what happened next.

---

The Institute holds, alongside the Oumar record, a set of field notebooks from the same period that arrived by a different route and document a different community, on a different continent, carrying knowledge of a recognisably similar kind.

The notebooks belong to Marta Elías Vega, a cultural anthropologist contracted in 2029 by the reconstituted Kvawd Corporation — the company the reader encountered in the previous chapter, now under different ownership and different leadership, pursuing what its new CEO described as "intelligence with integrity." The original Kvawd had been destroyed by the practices documented in NI-2142-1247. The brand and certain non-model assets were acquired in late 2028 by the Zhurong Group, a Shenzhen-based technology conglomerate, and relaunched as Kvawd Systems under the leadership of CEO Nadia Asante, a former DeepMind researcher. Asante's company achieved profitability within eleven months and, by mid-2030, operated the third-largest commercial language model by user count. The irony of what followed has been observed by others and requires no elaboration from the Institute.

Kvawd's project was called Fieldwork: the deployment of approximately 2,600 field researchers across 140 countries with the task of observing, recording, and documenting the aspects of human life that had never been rendered as text. The 2.8% of human knowledge that no model had ever been trained on. The knowledge that humans carry but do not write down. Asante's reasoning was simple and, within its own framework, unanswerable: every model had the same architecture, the same public data, the same compute. The company that understood *humans* — not language, not code, but the actual texture of human life as it was lived in the places where no webcrawl had ever reached — would win. The cost was $2.1 billion. The ambition was to capture what forty generations of pastoral knowledge and four hundred years of village life had produced, and to compress it into training data.

Vega was assigned to northern Castilla y León, in Spain. She wrote by hand, in a ruled Moleskine notebook, in a mixture of Spanish and English. Her handwriting was small, precise, and consistent throughout the fourteen months of entries. The notebooks were in excellent condition at the time of their donation to the Institute by her family in 2089, with the exception of a water stain on the cover of the second volume, which she noted in a margin was caused by a glass of tinto de verano in Soria.

What follows is drawn from those notebooks. The reader will find, in Vega's account of a village in Castilla, an echo of the knowledge Adama carried at the lake — distributed, undocumented, precise, and held in the bodies and habits of people who had never had reason to write it down. The Institute does not insist on the parallel. It simply places the documents side by side and leaves the resonance to the reader.

---

**11 March 2029 — Arriving**

The village has eighty-three registered inhabitants. The woman at the *ayuntamiento* — Lourdes, who is also the postal worker, also the person who holds the key to the church when the priest is not here, which is most of the time — told me this as if reporting a chronic illness. Eighty-three. Down from four hundred and twelve in 1970, she said, although she didn't need to say it because the number is painted on the wall of the municipal building next to a mural that someone commissioned in 2003 showing the village as it was in the 1950s: full streets, children, donkeys, laundry on lines between buildings. The mural is fading. Nobody has repainted it.

I have rented the upper floor of a house on Calle Mayor — the only street with a name, though it would be generous to call it a street. The house belongs to Tomás and Esperanza Ruiz, who are in their seventies and who asked no questions about why a woman from Madrid would pay €300 a month to live above them. Esperanza brought me a tortilla within an hour of my arrival. I had not asked. She had not announced she would. She simply knocked and handed it to me on a plate with a fork and said, *come, you must be hungry from the road*, and I stood in the doorway with a tortilla I had not asked for and understood, in a way that felt almost physical, that this was the first piece of data.

The tortilla was perfect. Runny in the centre, the way it should be. I ate it at the window looking out at a street that was empty except for a cat and an old man walking very slowly toward the bar.

I have been briefed extensively on what Kvawd wants. They want thick description. They want the texture of daily life in places where daily life has not been optimised, datafied, or made legible to algorithms. They want what they are calling, in their internal documents, "the human remainder" — the 2.8% that their models cannot access because no one has ever written it down.

I have a PhD in cultural anthropology from the Complutense. I have spent eleven years studying exactly this kind of knowledge — embodied practice, oral tradition, the structures of meaning that exist only in their performance. I have published papers on it. I have taught courses on it. And I have never, in my career, been asked to do it for the purpose of feeding it to a machine.

I am forty-five years old. I grew up in Vallecas, in Madrid, but my grandmother was from a village like this one, in Zamora, and I spent summers there until I was twelve, when she died and my parents stopped going. The village she came from no longer exists. Not abandoned — technically it still appears on maps — but the last permanent resident, a man named Aurelio who raised goats alone and spoke to no one, died in 2019. My grandmother's house has a tree growing through the kitchen.

I mention this because it is relevant to my motivation, which the project directors asked about in the interview and to which I gave a professional answer about the importance of documenting endangered lifeways. The real answer is: I already know what happens when no one writes it down.

---

**12 March 2029**

Morning routine in the village, observed from my window:

7:15 — Esperanza sweeps the front step. She does this every morning regardless of whether the step is dirty. It is not about the step.

7:40 — Three men walk to the bar. They walk separately, from three directions, but arrive within two minutes of each other. No one called anyone. No one arranged it. They simply know.

7:45 — The bar (Bar Manolo, though Manolo has been dead for nine years and the bar is now run by his daughter-in-law, also Lourdes, different Lourdes) opens. Coffee is served without being ordered. Each man receives the coffee he always receives. Nothing is written on a pad. Nothing is entered into a system.

8:30 — An older woman I have not yet identified walks the length of Calle Mayor carrying a bag of bread. She stops at four houses and leaves bread at each door without knocking. She does not collect money. I later learn from Esperanza that this is Pilar, that she bakes bread three times a week, and that the recipients pay her in *whatever they have* — sometimes money, sometimes eggs, sometimes nothing, sometimes a ride to Soria when they go. There is no ledger. No one keeps track. The system balances because everyone knows, in a way that has no mechanism, whether they owe or are owed.

I wrote three pages of field notes about the bread alone. Kvawd's briefing document says they want "granular behavioural description." I don't think they understand what they're asking for. The bread is not a behaviour. The bread is an economy, a social contract, a map of obligation, a daily performance of trust, and a woman's knees, which are bad. To describe it granularly is to need a hundred pages. To describe it accurately is to need zero, because the description is the bread itself, moving from house to house in the early morning, and the moment you write it down you have made it something else.

But that is what they are paying me for. So I write.

---

**24 March 2029 — The Funeral**

Agustín Delgado Ramos died yesterday evening. He was eighty-one. He had been ill — lungs, Esperanza told me, though she said it the way people here say things about illness, which is to say briefly and with a downward motion of the hand, as if pressing the information into the earth where it belongs. Agustín had been ill and now he was not ill, because he was dead, and the village was doing what the village does.

I learned about the death at 6:47 this morning. Not because anyone told me. I learned because the church bell rang — not the Sunday pattern, not the noon single toll, but a slow, spaced ringing that I had never heard but immediately understood. The bell tells you. The bell has always told you. And the sound of it — heavy, unhurried, final — carried across the valley and into every house, and I stood at my window and watched the village *change*. Not dramatically. Nothing dramatic happened. What happened was: shutters that had been open were closed halfway. A radio that had been playing was turned off. Pilar, who I could see at the far end of the street, stopped walking and stood still for the duration of the tolling. When the bell stopped, she crossed herself and continued, but more slowly.

Within an hour, the entire village knew. Not from phones. Not from messages. From the bell and from each other — short conversations at doorsteps, a hand on an arm, a nod. I watched the information travel from house to house like water finding its level. By 8:00, the bar was open but quiet. The men were there, at their usual table, but the television was off and they were speaking in low voices.

I should note, for the record that Kvawd is paying me to create, the following:

Agustín had no close family remaining in the village. His wife, Consuelo, died in 2021. His son lives in Barcelona and works in logistics. His daughter lives in Bilbao. I am told they have been called and are coming. The funeral will be tomorrow afternoon. The priest will come from Soria.

What I want to record is what happened between the bell and the funeral, because this is where the thing I cannot capture lives.

By noon, Esperanza had prepared food. Not for herself, not for me. For the house where Agustín died — for the neighbours who were sitting with the body, because someone is always sitting with the body; it is never alone between death and burial. She made a cazuela of lentils and a tortilla and she carried them down the street and she was not the only one. Three other women were doing the same thing, converging on the house from different directions, carrying pots. No one had assigned dishes. No one had coordinated. And yet there was no duplication. Esperanza brought lentils. Pilar brought croquetas. Another woman whose name I don't yet know brought a flan. The fourth brought bread and wine. Four women, four different dishes, no communication. I asked Esperanza how they knew who would bring what. She looked at me as if the question were slightly insane. *You just know*, she said. *You always know.*

I pressed. How? How do you know? She was patient with me, because she has decided I am basically a kind but confused person from the city, which is not entirely inaccurate.

*Pilar always makes croquetas when someone dies*, she said. *She has been making croquetas for funerals since her mother taught her. I make lentils because Tomás's mother always made lentils. Mercedes makes the flan. It has always been this way.*

*But what if someone new moved to the village?* I asked. *How would they know what to bring?*

She considered this with genuine seriousness, as if I had posed an interesting philosophical problem. *They would ask*, she said. *Or they would bring something and we would tell them, gently, what is already being brought, and they would bring something else next time. Within a year they would know.*

Within a year they would know.

I wrote that in my notebook and stared at it. This is a distributed knowledge system with no documentation, no formal protocol, no coordinator, and zero latency. It has operated without failure for longer than anyone can remember. It is more efficient, more resilient, and more adaptive than any recommendation algorithm I have encountered in my review of Kvawd's existing products. And it runs on four women who are between the ages of sixty-eight and eighty-one.

---

**25 March 2029 — The Funeral (cont.)**

The whole village went.

I need to say that again because I have written it and it looks, on the page, like a simple sentence, and it is not. The whole village went. Eighty-two people — eighty-three minus Agustín — walked to the church of San Martín at four o'clock in the afternoon. Some of them did not know Agustín well. Some of them did not like Agustín, because this is a village and villages contain everything, including grudges that have outlived the events that caused them. Old Fermín, who has not spoken to the Delgado family since a dispute over a boundary wall in 1987, put on a black jacket and walked to the church, and he stood at the back, and he stayed for the entire service, and afterward he shook hands with Agustín's son and said *lo siento* and meant it, or at least performed meaning it with a conviction that is indistinguishable from meaning it, which may be the same thing.

The church was cold. It is always cold. The priest, who drove from Soria and who clearly did not know Agustín beyond a name in a parish register, read the standard liturgy and said kind things that were true in the way that kind things said at funerals are true — generally, approximately, with goodwill. The village listened politely. They were not there for the priest.

What I observed, and what I am trying to render in language adequate to what Kvawd requires:

The seating was not random. It encoded the entire social structure of the village. Immediate neighbours of the deceased sat in the first rows. Close friends — meaning, in this context, men who had drunk coffee with Agustín every morning for decades — sat behind them. Other old men. Then the women who had cooked. Then everyone else, in an order that I could not fully parse but that clearly followed rules invisible to me and visible to everyone present. Old Fermín, with his thirty-six-year grudge, stood at the back by the door, which was itself a communication: I am here, I am respecting the dead, but I have not forgiven the living.

After the church, the procession to the cemetery. The whole village, walking. The coffin was carried by six men — four neighbours and two who had driven from other villages nearby, former friends of Agustín's.
