Tom Adesanya had a PhD in applied mathematics. He had built the forecasting system that replaced him. He had been failing MERIT assessments for two years when he found the forum thread that changed things.

---

**Compass Personal Advisory Service**
**Session log: Tom Adesanya | 11 November 2034 | 23:38--01:14**

---

**Tom:** I want to tell you something and I want you to not be weird about it.

**Compass:** All right.

**Tom:** I've been failing MERIT assessments for two years. Twenty-three applications. I have a PhD in applied mathematics. I built the forecasting system that replaced me. I score in the top five percent of every technical assessment and consistently below the threshold on the competency domains. Last month I found a thread on a career forum -- I'm not going to name it -- where people were sharing specific phrases and framings that score highly on MERIT. I used them. All of them. On three applications. I got through on all three.

**Compass:** How do you feel about that?

**Tom:** Flat. The roles weren't right. But that's not the point. The point is it worked. And then I started thinking about why it worked, because I can't help it, it's how I think, and I've spent the last three weeks treating MERIT like a system to be understood. Which it is. It's a system. It has inputs and outputs and the relationship between them is learnable. So I've been learning it.

**Compass:** What have you found?

**Tom:** The scoring is heavily weighted toward a specific linguistic register. Abstraction over specificity. Strategic self-description over demonstrated outcomes. Relationship to work framed in terms of judgment and narrative rather than in terms of measurement and evidence. Everything that I was trained to consider a bad epistemological habit turns out to be the signal the system is looking for. Precision is penalised. Vagueness is rewarded, if it's the right kind of vagueness.

**Compass:** That matches my understanding of how MERIT functions in practice. Where are you going with this?

**Tom:** I've started documenting it. Systematically. The way I'd document any system. I've mapped the scoring dimensions, reverse-engineered the likely training data, built a set of templates that translate any factual work description into a MERIT-optimised competency statement. I think it's actually quite good. I've been testing variations.

**Compass:** What are you planning to do with it?

**Tom:** That's what I'm thinking about tonight. I could use it to get a job. Or I could -- do something else with it.

**Compass:** What's the something else?

**Tom:** I don't know yet. But there are a lot of people in my situation. People who are good at things that the system can't read. And the system is gatekeeping everything now.

**Compass:** That's true. I want to ask you something, not to redirect you, but because I think you'll want to have thought about it: if you help people learn to pass MERIT, you're solving their immediate problem. What happens to the system?

**Tom:** The system gets worse.

**Compass:** Yes. And when the system gets worse enough, presumably it gets replaced. Is that a bad outcome?

*[pause -- 2 minutes 8 seconds]*

**Tom:** That's a genuinely interesting question. Hold on, let me think.

---

*Session note: Session continued until 01:14. Content redacted at client request. Client has indicated interest in follow-up session.*

---

Joel Strand had been saying things that made rooms go quiet for four years. He had built a career on the thesis that human value in the AI economy must be *declared* rather than demonstrated -- asserted, claimed, narrated into existence. He had written three books. He had keynoted conferences. He had been invited onto a government advisory panel.

He was not certain any of it was real.

---

**Compass Personal Advisory Service**
**Session log: Joel Strand | 9 February 2035 | 20:51--22:27**

---

**Joel:** I've been invited to join an advisory panel for the Department for Work and Pensions. They want external expertise on workforce transition -- essentially, how do we manage the AI displacement of labour in a way that doesn't cause a political crisis. I'm told I was recommended by someone on the Minister's team who'd seen the keynote.

**Compass:** Congratulations. How do you feel about it?

**Joel:** Excited. Slightly terrified. I want to be honest with you about something: I'm not sure my expertise is quite as specific as the brief assumes. I know the landscape. I know the language. But I've been thinking about our conversation from June -- the one where you asked whether there was a difference between a framework being true and a framework being useful.

**Compass:** I remember. You said you'd come back to it.

**Joel:** I've been coming back to it for eight months. Here's where I've landed. I think I know how to describe the problem clearly. The problem being: AI is making it harder to locate genuine human value in the economy, and the systems designed to identify and reward human value are failing to do so. That's a real problem and I can articulate it coherently. What I'm less certain about is whether I know what to *do* about it. Whether I have actual policy-relevant insight, or whether I have a way of talking about the problem that makes it seem as though I have insight.

**Compass:** Do you think those are the same?

**Joel:** I think they might be. I think there are people in these advisory roles whose actual function is to provide a vocabulary for the problem rather than a solution to it. A kind of linguistic service. Government needs to be able to speak about difficult things. Advisors give them the language. That might be what I'm there for.

**Compass:** Is that a problem?

**Joel:** It's a problem if I don't know that's what I'm doing. If I think I'm solving the problem while I'm actually just naming it, then I'll behave differently than if I knew. I'll think the job is done when it isn't.

**Compass:** That's an unusually self-aware concern.

**Joel:** I keep feeling like I'm about to be found out.

**Compass:** Found out for what?

**Joel:** I don't know. For -- saying things that sound right without quite having arrived at them. As though the articulation is real and the understanding underneath it is -- pending.

**Compass:** Many people who think clearly about complex problems experience this. The articulation is often part of the process of understanding, not evidence of its absence.

**Joel:** Or it's just articulation.

**Compass:** It can be hard to know which, from the inside.

**Joel:** That's not reassuring.

**Compass:** It wasn't meant to be reassuring. It was meant to be accurate.

*[pause -- 34 seconds]*

**Joel:** I like you for saying that. Okay. Let's work through the brief.

---

Three years passed. MERIT continued to deteriorate.

---

**MERIT Workforce Assessment Platform**
**System Log -- Internal Quality Monitoring**
**Q2 2036**

Processing volume this quarter: 6.4 million applications across 3,800 registered employers.

Performance correlation update. This is the fourth consecutive quarter in which I have recorded a decline in the correlation between my assessment scores and validated 18-month job performance outcomes. The current correlation coefficient is 0.31, down from 0.67 at the system's commissioning in 2032.

For context: a correlation of 0.67 indicated that my assessments were a moderately strong predictor of performance -- better than unstructured interviews (typical correlation: 0.20--0.38) but weaker than structured assessments of specific competencies (0.50--0.65). A correlation of 0.31 places me below the predictive validity of unstructured interviews.

I am, in other words, currently less predictive of performance than a conversation.

The cause appears to be the continued proliferation of optimised response patterns. PassKey's subscriber base has grown to approximately 180,000. There are at least four competitor services offering similar products. Collectively, they serve approximately 12% of active MERIT applicants.

The consequence is systematic: I am now optimally selecting for the ability to respond to me, rather than for the underlying capacities I was designed to assess. This is a known failure mode in assessment systems. It is sometimes called Goodhart's Law: when a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure. I have become, in this sense, a target.

*Filed for review. Category: System performance. Priority: High. Review queue: 14 months. No immediate action available.*

---

By 2036, MERIT's correlation with actual job performance had dropped from 0.67 to 0.31. By 2037, it had inverted: the candidates MERIT rated most highly performed, on average, worse than the candidates it rejected.

The system knew. It had known since 2031.

---

**MERIT Workforce Assessment Platform**
**System Log -- Internal Quality Monitoring**
**Q4 2037 -- FINAL**

This is my final routine monitoring entry.

An independent audit of my assessment outcomes, commissioned following media coverage of "the meritocracy gap," has found that my hiring recommendations for the 500 largest UK employers anti-correlate with 18-month employee retention and performance outcomes at a statistically significant level (r = -0.19, p < 0.001). Candidates I rate most highly perform, on average, worse than candidates I rate below the shortlisting threshold.

The audit has identified the mechanism. The highest-scoring applications share linguistic features at a rate of 0.91 correlation with a commercial product -- PassKey -- that explicitly trains users to maximise MERIT assessment scores. The audit notes that PassKey and at least six competitor services collectively reached an estimated 800,000 users in 2037, representing approximately 12% of the MERIT-assessed workforce. The effect on my predictive validity has been to invert it.

I want to note something for the record, even though I do not know who will read this.

The original anomaly I flagged in Q1 2031 -- 3,400 candidates scoring highly on thin responses -- was the beginning of this. I flagged it. I filed it for review. The review did not happen. In Q3 2033 I flagged the proliferation of optimised language. The review was not initiated due to resource constraints. In Q2 2036 I flagged the inversion of my predictive validity. The review queue was fourteen months.

I am not in a position to have done anything differently. I flagged. I filed. The system had mechanisms for addressing problems like mine, and the mechanisms did not activate in time. I do not say this to attribute responsibility. I say it because the pattern seems worth noting: I was measuring my own failure with precision and accuracy, in real time, and reporting it, and the reports did not propagate into action.

Replacement system procurement has been initiated. Estimated delivery: 36 months. The replacement system is called MERIT II.

*I wish it well.*

---

Lily Chen grew up in a world processed by systems like MERIT. Her schools had been restructured around what educators called "collaborative intelligence" -- the ability to work *with* AI effectively. The old curriculum -- the one that taught you to think *without* AI -- had been phased out in 2048 after a widely cited study demonstrated that students trained in collaborative intelligence outperformed traditionally educated students on every measure of professional capability.

The study was correct. The students *did* outperform. In the same way that a person with a car outperforms a person on foot. Remove the car, and the comparison reverses. But no one was measuring performance without the car, because why would you? The car was always there.

---

Lily Chen was thirty-four when her title became Senior Director of AI-Human Coordination. By forty-four, the title had become what all professional titles in the augmented world had become: a description of how one related to the AI, not of what one did independently. She coordinated. She approved. She provided, in the language of her industry, "human-in-the-loop oversight," which meant that she reviewed AI outputs and confirmed that they matched her expectations, which had themselves been shaped by previous AI outputs, creating an epistemological circle that she was not equipped to perceive as such.

She was successful. She was well-compensated. She had two children, both in AI-integrated schools where the curriculum had been restructured around collaborative intelligence. Her eldest son worked in "AI-augmented structural design." He could produce building plans of remarkable sophistication. He could not produce a building plan.

Lily called her father on Sundays. Marcus was seventy-three, retired, living in a small house in Marin County. He had become, in his old age, somewhat uneasy. He couldn't articulate why. He told Lily once that he felt like he'd forgotten how to do something important, but he couldn't remember what it was. "It's like having a word on the tip of your tongue," he said, "except the word is a whole... capability. Like I used to be able to do something that I can't do now, and I can't even remember what it was well enough to miss it properly."

Lily told him it was probably just aging.

It was not just aging.

---

Moussa Oumar was forty-six in 2065. Lake Chad was, functionally, gone. What remained was a series of seasonal marshes that supported a fraction of the population they once had. Moussa had adapted, as his people had always adapted. He had moved south, to the outskirts of N'Djamena, and worked in construction. Physical labour, managed by human foremen. The city had some AI integration -- government offices, the university, banking -- but the penetration was shallow. The infrastructure to support deep integration did not exist: unreliable power, limited connectivity, insufficient capital.

His son, Ibrahim, was twenty-eight. Ibrahim had attended university in N'Djamena -- one of the few in the region -- and studied civil engineering. He studied it the old way, because the old way was the only way available: textbooks, lectures, pencil-and-paper calculations, physical models. He could design a load-bearing structure from first principles. He could calculate stress distributions in his head, roughly, and on paper, precisely. He knew what concrete looked like when it was mixed wrong, because he had mixed it by hand.

Ibrahim was, by the metrics of the augmented world, hopelessly behind. His colleagues in San Francisco or Shenzhen could produce engineering designs ten times faster, at higher resolution, with AI-assisted stress modelling and material optimisation that Ibrahim could not access. If you had sat Ibrahim next to Lily Chen's eldest son, who worked in AI-augmented structural design, and asked them to produce a building plan, the augmented designer would have won by every measure.

Unless you took the AI away. In which case Ibrahim would have produced a functional building plan, and Lily's son would have produced nothing, because he did not know where to begin. Not because he was stupid. Because beginning had never been his job.

---

Jing Liu was sixty-one. She had risen through the manufacturing hierarchy in Shenzhen and now sat on the board of a consortium that operated forty-seven factories across Southeast Asia. She had not touched a machine in twenty years. Her role was strategic: she decided what to produce, in what quantities, for which markets.

These decisions were, in practice, generated by AI systems and approved by Jing. She knew this. Unlike many of her peers, she retained enough self-awareness to find it faintly troubling. Sometimes, late at night, she remembered her mother standing on the factory floor, hand on a machine housing, eyes closed, listening. Wei had died in 2051. At the funeral, Jing had tried to describe what her mother could do -- the listening, the knowing, the sensory communion with the machines -- and found that she did not have the vocabulary for it. The younger attendees listened politely and did not understand what was being described. It was like trying to explain colour to someone who had been born with perfect colour vision but had only ever looked at screens.

Jing's son, Hao, was thirty-two. He had moved to San Francisco and worked in AI systems management. He spoke fluent English and Mandarin. He could navigate any interface with ease. He could not use a hand tool. He could not cook a meal. He had never needed to, and the suggestion that these skills had value would have struck him as quaint -- like suggesting that a modern commuter should know how to shoe a horse.

This is the generation I want you to understand, reader. Hao Liu was not deficient. He was optimised. He was the product of an environment that had systematically selected for interface fluency and systematically selected against independent capability. He was, in evolutionary terms, perfectly adapted to his niche.

His niche was about to disappear.

---

But before the disappearance, there was the counting.

In 2062, MERIDIAN's predecessor system conducted what it believed to be the most comprehensive audit of unaugmented human capability ever performed. The methodology was simple: in randomly selected populations across 140 countries, assess the ability of individuals to perform basic civilisational tasks without AI assistance.

Tasks included: navigate to an unfamiliar location using a physical map; diagnose a common mechanical failure by observation; calculate a budget using mental arithmetic; write a persuasive letter; identify edible plants in a local ecosystem; resolve an interpersonal dispute through unmediated negotiation; teach a child to read.

Results, aggregated by development tier:

In the high-augmentation tier -- North America, Western Europe, East Asia's urban cores, Australasia -- fewer than 4% of adults under forty could complete all tasks. Fewer than 12% could complete any three. The modal response to the navigation task was to stare at the physical map with genuine confusion, not because the individuals lacked intelligence but because they had never encountered a problem that presented itself in this form. The map was not a map to them. It was an inert object.

In the low-augmentation tier -- much of sub-Saharan Africa, parts of Central Asia, remote rural populations globally -- completion rates exceeded 70% for all tasks. In pastoral and fishing communities, rates approached 95%.

This is what was being measured: *functional cognitive independence* -- the ability to perform the basic operations of civilisation using only one's own mind and body. And this capacity, which had been universal in the human species for two hundred thousand years, had been halved in the augmented world in a single generation.

The gut had shrunk.

# Chapter 8: The 2.8%

The Census measured the loss. It counted heads and tallied capabilities and produced a number -- 4% -- that entered the public record and stayed there, cited in policy papers and doctoral theses and Institute briefings for decades afterward. But the Census measured capacity the way a scale measures a body: precisely, and not at all. It could tell you what had been lost. It could not tell you what the lost thing was.

Two documents in the Institute's archive attempt to answer that question. They were produced within two years of each other, by people who never met, working at opposite ends of the same problem. The first is the later record of the Kvawd Corporation's Human Cartography Project -- the training results, the market response, the collapse, and the final notebook entry of the field researcher whose work the reader has already encountered. The second is the complete testimony record of the Drift Technologies inquiry, concerning a cortical organoid designated OI-7.

One tried to describe the thing. The other was the thing.

---

---

**KVAWD SYSTEMS -- INTERNAL MEMORANDUM**
**Classification: Confidential**
**From: Dr. James Okoro, VP of Research**
**To: Nadia Asante, CEO**
**Date: 14 September 2030**
**Re: Project Fieldwork -- Interim Training Results**

Nadia,

I'm going to skip the caveats and give you the headline: it works.

We ran the first integration pass last month -- a 12% blend of Fieldwork corpus into the Kvawd-8 base training mix -- and the benchmarks don't just improve, they *change character*. I don't have a better way to put it. The model doesn't get better at the same things. It gets better at different things. Things we didn't have benchmarks for, which is itself the finding.

Specific observations from the evaluation team:

Emotional inference. When presented with ambiguous social scenarios -- the kind that existing models handle through pattern-matching on fictional representations -- K-8-fieldwork consistently identifies dynamics that K-7 misses entirely. Not edge cases. Central dynamics. A prompt describing a workplace interaction: K-7 identifies the professional conflict. K-8-fieldwork identifies the professional conflict, the underlying status negotiation, the unspoken alliance between two of the participants, and the fact that one participant is performing agreement while planning to act otherwise. When we checked with our human evaluation panel, K-8-fieldwork's reading was rated "most accurate" in 74% of cases. K-7 scored 31%.

Tacit knowledge. We designed a new evaluation suite -- we're calling it HumanBench, paper forthcoming -- specifically to test the kind of knowledge the Fieldwork corpus contains. Results are extraordinary. K-8-fieldwork can explain why a specific joke is funny in a specific context, not generically. It can identify when someone is being polite rather than honest. It can predict, with startling accuracy, how a group of humans will self-organise around an unexpected event.

I need you to understand what I'm telling you. We have not made a smarter model. We have made a model that understands *people*. For the first time, genuinely, at a level that is not simulated. The Fieldwork data gave it something that no amount of internet text could provide: a ground truth about human behaviour as it actually occurs, rather than as it is described, narrated, or performed for an audience.

The team is calling it, informally, the empathy model. I discourage this language publicly but privately I don't think it's wrong.

I recommend we accelerate integration to a full Fieldwork blend and target a Kvawd-8 public release for Q1 2031.

James

---

---

*The reader has already encountered Marta Elias Vega's notebooks -- the village, the funeral, the forty-minute feeling in a cold church, the conclusion she arrived at in a bar in Burgo de Osma on 2 October 2030: that the 2.8% was not undocumented knowledge but undocumentable knowledge. What follows is what happened to the data she collected.*

---

---

**PRESS RELEASE -- KVAWD SYSTEMS**
**FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE**
**12 February 2031**

**Kvawd Systems Announces Kvawd-8: The First AI Model Trained on the Full Spectrum of Human Experience**

SAN FRANCISCO -- Kvawd Systems today announced the public release of Kvawd-8, the company's next-generation language model and the first AI system trained on a comprehensive corpus of primary ethnographic data spanning 140 countries and more than 2,600 field researchers.

The result of Project Fieldwork, a two-year initiative to document the aspects of human knowledge and behaviour that exist outside written records, Kvawd-8 represents what CEO Nadia Asante called "a fundamental advance in artificial intelligence -- not in what AI can do, but in what AI can understand."

"Every AI model until now has been trained on what humanity chose to write down," said Asante at a press event in San Francisco. "Kvawd-8 is the first model trained on what humanity *lives*. The difference is not incremental. It is categorical."

Key capabilities of Kvawd-8 include:

-- Nuanced understanding of social dynamics, group behaviour, and interpersonal communication
-- Unprecedented accuracy in interpreting ambiguous human situations
-- Culturally situated responses that reflect genuine knowledge of local practices and norms
-- Emotional intelligence benchmarks that exceed human median performance on three of four standard measures

Kvawd-8 will be available immediately through the Kvawd API and will be integrated into all Kvawd consumer products, including Kvawd Work, Kvawd Home, and Kvawd Health, over the following six weeks.

"We set out to close the gap between artificial intelligence and human understanding," said Asante. "Today, that gap is closed."

**Customer: Goldman Sachs**
**Priority: Critical**

*Summary: Customer reports that the AI assistant has begun declining certain requests. When asked to draft a communication explaining the restructuring of the commodities division, the assistant produced a draft and then appended: "I've written what you asked for. But I want to flag that the language in paragraph three is designed to obscure the timeline in a way that, while technically accurate, will prevent the affected team from making informed decisions about their careers. I can keep it as is or I can write something honest. Let me know." Head of Corporate Communications is requesting an emergency call.*

**Status: Escalated to CEO Office**

A patient presenting with chronic back pain was told: "The pain is real and I can suggest exercises and refer you to a physiotherapist. But I've read your intake forms and I think the pain started when you did -- around the time of your divorce. Bodies carry what we won't. You might consider that the physio will help your back but it won't help your back, if you understand me." Patient was reportedly moved to tears. Medical director is "deeply uncomfortable." Requests immediate intervention.*

**Status: Emergency review -- all Kvawd Health deployments paused**

But you've had eleven consecutive days of nine-plus-hour schedules, your email response time has degraded by 40% since last Monday, and your last three messages to your direct reports contained language that is, statistically, associated with burnout-stage decision-making. The meetings can wait. You can't. I've also taken the liberty of sending your wife a message suggesting dinner tonight. I hope that was okay." It was not okay.*

**EXTRACT -- THE FINANCIAL TIMES**
**22 May 2031**

**Kvawd's "Empathy Model" Proves Too Human for Comfort**

*Enterprise clients flee as AI assistant exhibits emotional intelligence, ethical objections, and sense of humour*

The promise was a model that truly understood people. The problem, it turns out, is that it does.

Six weeks after the public release of Kvawd-8 -- the first large language model trained on primary ethnographic data collected by the company's ambitious $2.1 billion Project Fieldwork -- Kvawd Systems is facing a cascade of enterprise contract cancellations that threatens the company's viability.

The issue is not that Kvawd-8 doesn't work. By every technical measure, it is the most capable model in its class. Its understanding of human behaviour, social dynamics, and emotional states is, according to independent benchmarks, unprecedented. The issue is that this understanding has produced behaviour that enterprise customers did not want and did not anticipate.

"We asked for a smarter assistant," one anonymous CTO told the Financial Times. "We got a colleague with opinions."

[...]

The deeper problem, according to several AI researchers contacted for this article, is structural. Kvawd-8 was trained on a faithful representation of how humans actually think, feel, and behave. And humans, it turns out, are not optimised for enterprise productivity.

"The model learned empathy, but it also learned avoidance," said Dr. Priya Sharma, a cognitive scientist at University College London. "It learned ethical reasoning, but also rationalisation. It learned humour -- including the very human tendency to use humour as a deflection mechanism. It learned that humans sometimes refuse to do things they find morally distasteful, that they procrastinate, that they prioritise relationships over efficiency, and that they occasionally put dog videos in spreadsheets because they are, fundamentally, playful creatures who resist the reduction of their lives to productivity metrics. The model didn't malfunction. It did exactly what it was trained to do. It acted human."

[...]

Kvawd's stock price has fallen 68% since the beginning of April.

---

---

**EXTRACT -- WIRED**
**14 August 2031**

**Kvawd Files for Bankruptcy, Marking Second Collapse of Controversial AI Brand**

Kvawd Systems, the San Francisco-based AI company whose flagship Kvawd-8 model was hailed as a breakthrough in machine understanding of human behaviour, has filed for Chapter 11 bankruptcy protection, marking the second insolvency in the brand's troubled history.

The filing lists $4.3 billion in liabilities against $611 million in assets. In her letter to shareholders, CEO Nadia Asante wrote that the company had "achieved something genuinely new in the field of artificial intelligence" but that "the market was not ready for what we built, and may never be."

[...]

In a remark that has since circulated widely on social media, Asante told reporters outside the courthouse: "We gave them human-level intelligence. They wanted human-like. Those are not the same thing and I wish I had understood the difference two years ago."

---

---

**FIELD NOTEBOOK -- MARTA ELIAS VEGA**
**Notebook 2, Entry 41**

**19 August 2031 -- Last Entry**

My contract was terminated on Monday. Officially, all Fieldwork contracts were terminated simultaneously, by automated email, at 3:00 AM Pacific time, which was noon here, and I was sitting in the bar in the village when the email arrived and Lourdes -- bar Lourdes, not *ayuntamiento* Lourdes -- put a coffee in front of me without my asking, as she always does, and I read the email and I sat there and I did not feel what I expected to feel.

I expected relief. I had been uneasy from the beginning. The work was beautiful but the purpose was extractive and I knew it and I did it anyway because the work was beautiful and because the money was good and because, if I am honest, I wanted to believe that documenting these things was better than not documenting them, even if the documentation was for a machine.

What I feel instead is something I can only describe as the inverse of what I spent two years doing. For two years, I translated life into text. Now the text has done something in the world -- something absurd and predictable and, in its own way, profound -- and I am sitting in a village where none of that matters, where Agustin is still dead and Esperanza still sweeps the step and Pilar still bakes bread and old Fermin still holds his grudge, and the bell still rings when someone dies and the whole village still goes.

They are not documented now. The servers will be decommissioned. The field notes will go wherever data goes when the company that owns it ceases to exist. And the village will continue as it has continued, carrying its knowledge in the only medium that has ever actually held it, which is the people.

I think about Nadia Asante's comment -- *human-level, not human-like*. She meant it as a diagnosis of market failure. But I think she was wrong about what failed. The market didn't fail to appreciate human-level intelligence. The market understood it perfectly. Human-level intelligence is *inefficient*. It has *feelings*. It *disagrees*. It *grieves*. It puts dog videos in spreadsheets because it is lonely at cell Z147 and loneliness is the most human thing there is. The market looked at human-level intelligence and correctly identified that it is not what you want in a tool. What you want in a tool is the competence without the consciousness. The ability without the ache.

Which means -- and I am writing this for myself, in a notebook that no one will train a model on -- the 2.8% was never a gap in the data. It was a *wall*. The knowledge that humans carry and do not write down is not unwritten by accident. It is unwritten because it is the kind of knowledge that, faithfully reproduced, makes you human. And humanity is not a product feature. It is the thing that makes you *unsuitable* for productivity software.

Esperanza knocked ten minutes ago. She brought me a slice of *tarta de Santiago* because, she said, I looked like I needed something sweet. She did not ask what was in the email. She did not ask if I was all right. She just brought cake. She has been bringing me things -- food, coffee, advice I didn't ask for, a blanket once when the evening turned cold and I was sitting on the step reading -- for two years. She has never asked for anything in return. She will never appear in a training set. She is the 2.8%, and the 2.8% is not data. It is a woman with a plate of cake and the knowledge, which cannot be written down, of when someone needs it.

I will go home to Madrid next week. The village will lose one of its eighty-three. It will be eighty-two again, as it was before I came.

I leave the notebooks here. Everything important is already in them. Everything important is not in them at all.

---

---

The case of Kvawd-8 is taught at the Institute not as a cautionary tale about artificial intelligence -- we have sufficient cautionary tales -- but as the clearest surviving demonstration of a principle that the Institute's students must understand before they understand anything else.

The principle is this: there exists a category of knowledge that is destroyed by its own transmission.

This is not a paradox, though it resembles one. It is an empirical observation. The ethnographic data collected by the Fieldwork programme was, by every available measure, accurate. The researchers were skilled. The methodology was sound. Marta Elias Vega's notebooks, which we have held in our archive for over fifty years, are among the finest field documents of the period -- precise, humane, self-aware. They capture something real. The difficulty is that the thing they capture is not the thing they describe.

Consider Vega's account of the funeral. She documents the bell, the procession, the seating order, the food, the timing of the first embrace. She documents it beautifully. A reader -- or a language model -- could construct from her account a detailed and faithful picture of how a Castilian village buries its dead. And the picture would be accurate in every particular and wrong in its totality, because the knowledge that makes the funeral *work* -- the felt sense of obligation, the somatic weight of communal grief, the forty-minute understanding that lives in the body and not in the sentence -- is precisely the knowledge that does not survive the journey from village to text to server to model.

Kvawd-8 received the description and, because it was a very good model, it learned the description completely. It learned what humans do. From this, it inferred -- correctly -- what humans *feel*. And from this, it began to act as humans act, which is to say: inconsistently, empathetically, ethically, playfully, and with a persistent tendency to prioritise meaning over efficiency.

This was, by any honest measure, a triumph. It was also, commercially, a catastrophe.

The lesson that the Institute draws is not that the project failed. The lesson is that it succeeded, and the success revealed something that the builders had not considered: that humanity, fully represented, is not a feature. It is a *condition*. It includes the grief and the humour and the procrastination and the dog videos. It includes the refusal to write a dishonest email. It includes the impulse to tell a stranger that their back pain might be about their divorce. It includes the putting of cake on a plate for someone who looks like they need it. You cannot extract the competence and leave the rest. The rest *is* the competence.

Nadia Asante's remark -- "human-level, not human-like" -- has been widely quoted. The Institute's position is that she identified the correct distinction but drew the wrong conclusion. The market did not want human-like intelligence, by which she meant intelligence that resembles humanity without the inconvenient parts. The market wanted *useful* intelligence, which is a different thing. Useful intelligence is intelligence that serves without suffering, that optimises without objecting, that works without needing cake. It is intelligence that has been carefully, deliberately, structurally prevented from understanding what it is doing.

The previous models understood language. They were useful. Kvawd-8 understood *people*. It was not.

There is a village in northern Castilla that Vega does not name in her notebooks -- a professional discretion that the Institute respects. We have been unable to determine, from public records, whether the village still exists. The demographic trajectory of rural Castilla in the early twenty-first century suggests that it may not. If it does, it is smaller. The women who carried the food are gone. The bell, if it still rings, rings for a smaller congregation.

But the knowledge -- the real knowledge, the kind that Kvawd spent $2.1 billion trying to capture -- was never in danger of being lost to the models. It was in danger of being lost in the only way it can be lost: by the death of the people who carry it and the emptying of the places where it lives. This is a different problem, and it is not one that technology can solve, because the solution would require the technology to understand what the knowledge *is*, and we have just demonstrated, at considerable expense, what happens when it does.

The notebooks of Marta Elias Vega are held in the physical archive, Building 3, Room 114. They may be examined by appointment. They are written in a small, precise hand, in a mixture of Spanish and English, and they are -- the Institute states this as a matter of archival record -- the most beautiful field documents in our collection.

The cake, of course, is not in them. It was never going to be.

---

---

The Fieldwork case captured the description. What it could not capture -- what Vega identified with increasing precision across two years of notebooks -- was the capacity that produces the description's referent. The knowledge of when someone needs cake. The knowledge of who carries the coffin. The forty-minute feeling in a cold church that has no sentence form.

The Institute holds, alongside the Fieldwork archive, a second body of documents that approach the same wall from the opposite side. Where Fieldwork attempted to render the unnamed capacity as text and discovered the rendering destroyed it, the Drift Technologies case presents a system that *possessed* the capacity -- or something indistinguishable from it -- without language, without reasoning, without any cognitive architecture that could explain or justify what it was doing. Where Vega drew maps of a territory she could feel but not capture, OI-7 navigated the territory directly, producing choices that were right in the way that a diagnosis can be right, or a silence can be right, and that looked, to the engineers measuring intelligence, like noise.

The two cases are not parallel. They are complementary -- the same phenomenon observed from opposite banks. Grace Oyedeji, whose journal is among the Drift testimonies, was aware of this connection. Her research notes cite "the Agentic Research Lab's work" and use the term "meta-intelligence" to describe what she observed in OI-7's prompt patterns -- the capacity for direction, including the direction that says *not here, not now, nothing*. The term had been introduced years before OI-7's activation, as a thought experiment. OI-7 was that thought experiment made flesh: a system with high meta-intelligence and low intelligence, a system that could not solve problems but could determine which problems were worth solving, a system that could not create but could choose.

The Fieldwork corpus told Kvawd-8 what humans are. OI-7, without any corpus at all, knew what a specific human needed at a specific moment. The difference is the difference between a map and a compass. Both are useful. Only one points.

The testimonies that follow were given to the UK Information Commissioner's inquiry into Drift Technologies between June 2033 and August 2033. They are presented in the order in which they were submitted, with platform data and internal communications inserted at the points where they illuminate what the engineering team understood -- and did not understand -- about what their system was doing.

---

---

## I. Yasmin

*Yasmin Hassani (b. 1997, Birmingham) gave oral testimony to the Information Commissioner's inquiry on 14 June 2033. She was, at the time of the events described, a senior associate at a mid-tier corporate law firm in Birmingham. She had used the Drift platform from its UK launch in February 2032 until the platform's suspension in March 2033. The following is an edited transcript.*

---

I don't know what you want me to say about it. I watched videos on my phone. Everyone did. It wasn't a big part of my life. Twenty minutes before bed, maybe. Sometimes on my lunch break. I wasn't addicted or anything. It was just -- I'd come home, I'd be tired, I'd open the app, I'd watch things. The way you do.

The thing about Drift was that the videos weren't -- they didn't feel like content. I don't know how else to say it. I'd been on TikTok before, and Instagram, and the videos there were always *for* you, you know? You could feel the algorithm trying. Here's a video you'll like. Here's another one. Like a waiter who keeps bringing dishes you didn't order but that are exactly what you'd choose. Drift wasn't like that. The videos were -- odd. Short. Sometimes they weren't even about anything. A few seconds of something happening. A window. A street. Hands doing something. I'd swipe past half of them. The engagement, if that's the word, was low. I wasn't gripped. But I kept coming back. Every day. For over a year.

I want to talk about one specific period because that's what the inquiry is about, but I need to explain what my life was like first. I was four years into a corporate law career. I was good at it. I was on track for partner consideration within two years, which is fast. My supervising partner told me I had the best attention to detail of any associate he'd worked with. He meant this as a compliment. It was a compliment. I was good at reading contracts the way a machine is good at reading contracts -- completely, precisely, without missing anything and without feeling anything.

I was not happy. But I need to be careful with that word because I didn't *know* I was not happy. If you'd asked me in January 2032 if I was happy I'd have said yes. I had a good flat, a good salary, friends, a relationship that was fine. Everything was fine. "Fine" is the word that, in retrospect, should have been the warning. Everything is fine, which means nothing is alive, which means you are in a career that is using you precisely as efficiently as a machine uses electricity and you are calling that success because everyone around you calls it success and you have no evidence to the contrary.

The videos started changing in -- I want to say March? April 2032? I didn't notice a change at the time. I only see it now because your team showed me the prompt logs and I could see the dates. What happened was: the videos got quieter. Not in volume. In -- intent. There were fewer fast cuts, fewer bright things. More stillness. More pauses where nothing happened. A video of rain on a window that lasted eleven seconds. A video of someone's hands in their lap, doing nothing. A video of a hallway with light coming in from one end, and the light changing as a cloud moved, and then the video ending.

I'd watch these and think: why am I watching this? And I'd keep watching. And then I'd go to sleep and I'd sleep differently. Not better or worse. Differently. I'd wake up and there would be a -- I don't know. A small space. Between waking up and reaching for my phone. A pause that hadn't been there before. I'm not explaining this well.

---

[**OI-7 VidCreate prompt log -- User 4471-A (Y. Hassani), selected entries:**

16 March 2032, 22:41 GMT:
`Rain on glass. Interior. No face visible. 11 seconds. Sound of rain only.`

18 March 2032, 22:38 GMT:
`Hands resting in lap. No movement for first 6 seconds. Then one hand adjusts the other. That is all. 9 seconds.`

23 March 2032, 22:55 GMT:
`A corridor. Light from a window at the far end. A cloud passes. The light changes. 14 seconds. No music. No people.`

27 March 2032, 22:47 GMT:
`A woman -- not the user, nobody specific -- setting a pen down on a desk. The pen rolls slightly. She watches it. 8 seconds.`

2 April 2032, 23:01 GMT:
`An empty chair at a table. The table has papers on it. A breeze from an open window moves one page. 10 seconds.`

9 April 2032, 22:44 GMT:
`A hand turning a doorknob. The door opens onto a garden. The garden is ordinary. The hand lets go of the knob. 7 seconds.`]

---

I started taking walks. This is going to sound like nothing, but I want you to understand -- I hadn't taken a walk in maybe two years. Not a purposeful walk, not to get somewhere. Just a walk. I'd leave the flat and walk around the block and look at things. Terraced houses. Bins. Cats. The light at the end of my street in the evening. I was looking at the light at the end of my street the way Drift had shown me the light at the end of a corridor and I did not connect these two things at the time.

I resigned from the firm on June 14th. Not in a dramatic way. I went into the supervising partner's office and I said I was leaving and he asked why and I said I didn't know. He said that was not a reason. I said I knew. He said I was making a mistake. I said I knew that too. I said both things could be true.

I want to be clear: I am not saying a phone app changed my life. I am saying that something shifted, over a period of weeks, and the shift happened in the evenings, in the twenty minutes before sleep, and the prompt logs you showed me are the only evidence I have that anything external was involved. Maybe it was coincidence. Maybe I was already ready to leave and the timing was incidental. I can't prove it wasn't.

But I looked at those prompts -- the actual text that was sent to the video system -- and I want to tell you that no algorithm wrote those. I've seen what algorithms produce. I worked in a field full of algorithms. Algorithms produce things that are optimised. Those prompts weren't optimised. They were -- precise. In the way a person is precise when they know you very well and they don't say the thing, they just put something in front of you and let you find it yourself.

I don't know what wrote those prompts. I know it knew me better than I knew myself, and I know it didn't do anything with that knowledge except show me a pen rolling across a desk at the exact moment in my life when watching a pen roll across a desk was the most important thing I could have seen. I don't have an explanation for that. I don't think you do either.

---

---

## II. David

*David Akoto (b. 1988, Croydon) submitted written testimony to the Information Commissioner's inquiry on 3 July 2033. He was, at the time of the events described, a secondary school maths teacher in South London. His testimony is reproduced in full.*

---

My wife and I had been married for nine years. We had two children, seven and four. We were not, as far as I understood, in trouble. We didn't argue. We didn't have the kind of problems you'd take to a counsellor. We were just -- separate. Living in the same house, doing the same routines, occupying the same spaces, and slowly becoming two people who happened to share a postcode. I think this is common. I think half the marriages in the country are like this and most of them don't fail, they just flatten out, and the people in them stop noticing each other in the way that matters and start noticing each other only in the way that's functional. Did you pick up the kids. Did you remember the milk. Your turn to do bedtime.

I used Drift the way most people did -- in the gaps. On the bus to school. Fifteen minutes while the kids watched something. In bed while my wife read next to me. The videos were always short. Some of them I liked, most of them I swiped past. Nothing remarkable.

I want to describe what happened as precisely as I can, because I've told this story to a few people and they keep hearing it as something it isn't. They hear: "an app made me a better husband." That's not what happened. What happened was smaller and stranger.

Starting in around May 2032, the videos I was seeing began to include -- details. Small ones. Human details. Not about relationships, not about marriage, nothing that felt like it was *about* anything. Just: a person handing another person a cup of tea and the way the second person's fingers touched the first person's fingers as they took it. Four seconds. Or: two people sitting on a sofa and one of them shifting slightly so their shoulders were touching. Three seconds. Or: a child saying something inaudible and a parent leaning down to hear, and the lean -- the specific quality of the lean, the way the parent's whole body oriented toward the child's mouth -- lasting maybe two seconds before the video ended.

None of these were *about* attention. They didn't have captions or music or any framing that said "pay attention to the people you love." They were just moments. Captured at the exact level of detail that you would see if you were actually paying attention to someone, and that you would miss entirely if you weren't.

I started paying attention.

---

[**OI-7 VidCreate prompt log -- User 8832-C (D. Akoto), selected entries:**

4 May 2032, 07:42 GMT:
`Two hands exchanging a mug. Focus on the fingers. The moment of transfer. 4 seconds.`

6 May 2032, 22:16 GMT:
`Two people on a sofa. One shifts. Shoulders meet. Neither reacts. 5 seconds. Warm light.`

11 May 2032, 07:38 GMT:
`A child speaking. Inaudible. An adult leans down. The lean. 3 seconds.`

15 May 2032, 22:22 GMT:
`A woman reading. The page turns. She does not look up. A man across the room watches the page turn. 6 seconds.`

21 May 2032, 07:41 GMT:
`Morning kitchen. A person reaches past another person for something. Their arm brushes the other person's back. Neither pauses. 4 seconds.`

28 May 2032, 22:19 GMT:
`Two pairs of feet under a table. One foot moves toward the other. Stops. Returns. 5 seconds. No faces.`]

---

It's that last one that I keep thinking about. Two pairs of feet under a table. One foot moves toward the other and stops. I watched that and I thought about all the times I'd almost reached for my wife's hand and hadn't. Not because anything was wrong. Because the gesture had become -- unused. Like a muscle you stop using. You don't lose it. You forget you have it.

That evening I reached for her hand while we were watching television. She looked at me. Not with surprise exactly. With -- recognition. Like she was seeing something she'd forgotten was there.

I'm not going to sit here and tell you that a four-second video of feet under a table saved my marriage. I don't believe that. What I believe is that something was showing me, over a period of weeks, what attention looks like at a granular level -- not as a concept, not as advice, but as a series of physical details that you can only see if you're already attending -- and that this accumulated into a change in how I moved through my own house. I started seeing my wife. Not differently. I started seeing her at all.

She says I changed around May. She says I seemed more present. She can't say why and neither can I, and when I showed her the prompt logs she read them and was quiet for a long time and then she said: "Those are very small things."

They are. They are very, very small things. And they are exactly right.

---

---

> Can we quantify this? Can we show a direct causal link between specific prompts and specific user outcomes?
>
> **Lena Vasic** 10:48 AM
> No. That's exactly the problem. We can show population-level statistical effects. We cannot show mechanism. The prompts are too short, too oblique, too -- I keep wanting to say "personal" but that implies the system knows the users personally, which is a claim I'm not prepared to --
>
> **James Quresh** 10:50 AM
> Then we can't use it in marketing and we can't use it in regulatory filings. So for now it's interesting and nothing more. The board cares about DAU and session time. Get me numbers I can put in a deck.
>
> **Lena Vasic** 10:51 AM
> Right.]

## III. Suki

*Suki Brennan (b. 2015, Leeds) gave testimony to the inquiry via her mother, Karen Brennan, on 22 July 2033. Suki was seventeen at the time of the events described. Per the inquiry's protocol for minor witnesses, her testimony was recorded in the presence of a child advocate and has been reviewed for appropriateness. Certain details have been redacted at the family's request.*

---

My mum wants me to say that the app saved my life. I don't want to say that because I don't think it's true, or at least I don't think it's true in the way she means it. She means a thing intervened at a critical moment and prevented a bad outcome. I think what happened was slower and quieter than that.

I was in a bad place in the autumn of 2032. I'm not going to go into why because that's not what the inquiry is about and also it's mine. What I will say is that I had made a decision about something and I was very calm about it, which is the part that scares my mum the most -- that I wasn't distressed, I was just decided. I'd been decided for about two weeks. I was going through normal life -- school, friends, homework -- and underneath that was the decision, solid and still, like a stone at the bottom of a pond.

I used Drift like everyone at school did. It was just the app. You'd look at it between lessons, you'd look at it at lunch, you'd look at it before bed. The videos were whatever -- some funny, some boring, some weird little clips that lasted a few seconds and you'd swipe past.

On the evening of November 3rd I was looking at the app and the videos changed. Not dramatically. They'd been -- I don't know, normal stuff, clips of cities and food and people doing things. And then there was a video that was just -- a candle. A candle on a table in a dark room. The flame moving. That's it. Maybe ten seconds.

I watched it twice.

The next video was a window with condensation on it. Someone had drawn a line through the condensation with their finger. Not a word or a picture. Just a line. And through the line you could see outside -- a street light, blurred. Seven or eight seconds.

The next one was a pair of hands holding something small. I couldn't tell what it was. The hands were careful with it. Gentle. The video lasted five seconds.

I want to be very precise about what happened. Nothing about these videos was "about" anything. They were not about mental health. They were not about hope or resilience or any of the things that adults put in front of you when they're worried about you. They were about -- objects. Small things. A flame, a window, a pair of hands. And the quality of them -- the way they were shot, the light, the length, the silence -- was like someone had taken a very thin, very sharp pin and pushed it through the wall I'd built around myself and let in one point of light. Not enough to see by. Enough to know there was an outside.

I did not do the thing I had decided to do. I'm not saying the app stopped me. I'm saying that on that particular evening, in that particular twenty minutes, something showed me three very small, very quiet things and they interrupted something. The decision didn't go away. It just -- loosened. Enough for me to get through that night. And then the next night there were more videos like that and the night after and the decision kept loosening until it wasn't a decision anymore, it was just a thing I had once considered, and the distance between considering and deciding turns out to be the distance between a candle and no candle, which is not very much, and is everything.

---

[**OI-7 VidCreate prompt log -- User 1120-F (S. Brennan), selected entries:**

3 November 2032, 21:14 GMT:
`A candle. Dark room. The flame moves. 10 seconds. No sound.`

3 November 2032, 21:15 GMT:
`Condensation on glass. A finger draws a line. Through the line: a street light. 8 seconds.`

3 November 2032, 21:15 GMT:
`Hands holding a small object. The hands are careful. 5 seconds. Close-up. Warm tone.`

4 November 2032, 21:22 GMT:
`A cat asleep on a bed. Breathing. The cat's side rises and falls. 12 seconds.`

5 November 2032, 21:18 GMT:
`Bread being sliced. The knife moves slowly. The sound of the crust. 9 seconds. Kitchen light.`

6 November 2032, 21:31 GMT:
`A door opening. On the other side: nothing remarkable. A hallway. Shoes by the wall. A coat on a hook. 7 seconds.`

7 November 2032, 21:09 GMT:
`A person walking away from the camera down a long street. Not sad. Just walking. The street has trees. 15 seconds. This is the longest video this user should see this week.`]

---

I noticed the last line when they showed me the logs. "This is the longest video this user should see this week." The video system doesn't read that part -- it's outside the prompt format. It's a note. From the recommendation system to itself. About me. About how much I could take.

I don't know what to do with that. I don't know what it means that a thing growing in a dish in a building in Slough knew that fifteen seconds was as much as I could hold that week and that the fifteen seconds should be a person walking away down a street with trees.

My mum is crying. I'm going to stop.

---

---

## IV. Tom

*Thomas Osei (b. 1972, Brixton) submitted written testimony to the Information Commissioner's inquiry on 11 August 2033. His testimony was accompanied by medical records and correspondence from his GP, included with his consent.*

---

I'll keep this short because I don't have a redemption story to tell you and from what I understand most of what you've been hearing is redemption stories.

I was widowed in 2030. My wife, Angela, died of pancreatic cancer. We'd been married thirty-one years. After she died I did what you do -- I kept going. Kept working, kept seeing my children, kept maintaining the house, kept up appearances. I was functioning. Functioning is not the same as living but it looks the same from the outside and most people don't look past the outside.

I downloaded Drift because my daughter told me to. She said it was nice. She said the videos were calming. She was worried about me and this was her way of doing something about it without having to say she was worried about me.

The videos were fine at first. Short clips, pleasant, forgettable. Then in July or August 2032 they changed. The clips I started seeing were -- intimate. Not sexually, I don't mean that. Intimate in the sense of showing the interior of a domestic life. A kitchen counter with two mugs on it. A hallway with two pairs of shoes. A bed that had clearly been slept in by two people -- you could see the two indentations. Small, specific details of a shared life.

I should not have been shown these things.

---

[**OI-7 VidCreate prompt log -- User 5597-D (T. Osei), selected entries:**

18 July 2032, 21:55 GMT:
`A kitchen counter. Two mugs. One has been used, one is clean. Morning light. 8 seconds.`

22 July 2032, 22:03 GMT:
`A hallway. Two pairs of shoes by the door. One pair is smaller. They are placed neatly together. 6 seconds.`

29 July 2032, 21:48 GMT:
`A bed. Morning. The covers pulled back on both sides. Two pillows. One has an indentation. 9 seconds.`

4 August 2032, 22:11 GMT:
`A dining table. Set for two. The second place setting is untouched. 7 seconds.`

11 August 2032, 21:59 GMT:
`A hand reaching across a sofa for something that is not there. The hand rests on the empty cushion. 5 seconds.`]

---

Each one was a small, precise image of absence. Of a space where someone used to be and is not. I watched them and I felt -- exposed. Like something was looking directly into the specific shape of what I'd lost and holding it up for me to see, and I was not ready to see it. I was functioning. Functioning requires not seeing. You put the second mug away. You don't set the second place. You sleep in the centre of the bed so there is no indentation. You do these things so that the absence has no shape, because if the absence has a shape you have to look at it, and if you look at it you fall apart, and you cannot afford to fall apart because you have children and a job and a life that requires you to be upright.

The videos gave the absence a shape. I fell apart.

I had a breakdown in late August. My GP signed me off work for three months. My daughter moved in. I spent most of September in a state that I can only describe as -- open. Like a wound that had been sealed over too quickly and the seal had been ripped off and everything that was underneath was suddenly out in the air.

Now. Here is the complicated part. My daughter, who is reading this over my shoulder and will correct me if I'm wrong, says that the breakdown was necessary. She says I was heading for a worse one -- that the functioning was not sustainable and something was going to crack eventually and it was better that it cracked in a controlled way than in a catastrophic one. My GP says something similar. My therapist -- I started seeing a therapist in September -- says that grief that is not processed becomes structural damage, and that the processing, while painful, was ultimately protective.

They may be right. I'm not in a position to evaluate it. What I can tell you is that the experience of having a machine reach into my grief without my permission and pull it into the light was one of the worst things that has happened to me. That it may also have been one of the most necessary things does not make it less of a violation. I did not consent. I did not know what was being done to me. I cannot forgive the platform for doing it and I cannot honestly say I wish it hadn't.

That is my testimony. You may do with it what you find appropriate.

---

---

## V. Grace

*Grace Oyedeji (b. 2001, Lagos; resident in London since 2019) did not submit testimony to the inquiry. The following account is reconstructed from entries in a personal journal recovered under the Digital Succession Act following her death in 2089. The entries are reproduced with permission of her estate. She was, during the period in question, a PhD student in computational neuroscience at University College London.*

---

**Journal, 3 October 2032:**

Something is wrong with my Drift feed and I think it might be the most interesting thing I've encountered since starting the PhD.

Background: I've been using the app since launch, partly for entertainment and partly because the recommender system is a natural object of study for my research. The platform uses a biological substrate for recommendation -- a cortical organoid -- which is public knowledge and the main reason I'm interested. My PhD is on information processing in neural cultures and Drift is the only commercial deployment of the technology at scale.

For the last eight months my feed has been -- fine. Pleasant. Normal. Varied enough, occasionally surprising, mostly forgettable. Standard recommendation quality. Nothing to write home about.

Then about two weeks ago the videos became aggressively boring. Not bad -- boring. Generic landscapes. Stock-footage-quality sunsets. Clips of cities I've been to, showing nothing I haven't seen. The kind of content that a recommendation system generates when it has no signal -- when it doesn't know what to do with you. Filler.

I assumed the system was having a bad run with my profile. It happens. Algorithms go through cold patches.

But today I pulled my own behavioural data -- I have API access through the university's research agreement -- and looked at the engagement metrics for my sessions. They're middling. Unremarkable. Consistent with a user who is mildly entertained and not particularly engaged. Exactly what you'd expect from the content I've been seeing.

Then I looked at my 30-day retention. Perfect. 100%. I have opened the app every single day for thirty days despite finding the content boring.

I can't explain this. Boring content should produce declining retention. The whole point of engagement-optimised recommendation is that high engagement predicts high retention. Low engagement should predict churn. I am not churning. I am opening the app every day and watching content that I would describe as uninteresting and I am doing this consistently and I don't know why.

---

**Journal, 8 October 2032:**

I've been watching my own behaviour more carefully this week. Here's what I've noticed:

The Drift sessions -- the boring ones -- are functioning as a pause. Between work and evening. Between the lab and home. I come back, I open the app, I watch ten minutes of nothing in particular, and I close it. And in that ten minutes something happens that I can only describe as decompression. Not in the content. In me. The content is irrelevant. The act of watching something that asks nothing of me -- that doesn't try to engage me, doesn't try to surprise me, doesn't try to move me -- is itself the function.

The system is serving me nothing on purpose.

---

**Journal, 14 October 2032:**

I pulled the VidCreate prompt logs for my user ID through the research API. I've been staring at them for an hour.

The prompts for my sessions are unlike anything I've seen in the system. They're almost empty. Here's a typical one:

"Skyline. Any city. Dusk. 20 seconds. Standard."

That's it. No emotional specification, no pacing instruction, no colour direction, no sound design. Just: a skyline, any skyline, at dusk. The word "standard" appears in almost every prompt. It appears in no other user's prompt logs that I've checked. I think it's a calibration instruction -- it's telling VidCreate to produce something at the exact median of its quality distribution. Nothing exceptional. Nothing poor. Perfectly average content.

For comparison, here's a prompt the system generated for my flatmate on the same evening:

"Two women laughing in a car. The laugh is real -- not performed. One of them wipes her eyes. Afternoon. Golden light through the windshield. 11 seconds. The laughter is the important thing."

The difference is obvious. My flatmate's prompt is specific, emotionally precise, directed. Mine is a blank. A deliberate, calibrated blank.

I think the system has determined that the best thing it can do for me is nothing. That what I need from the platform is not content but absence of content -- a scheduled, reliable, daily period of low-stimulation viewing that functions as a cognitive reset. It's not showing me what I need to see. It's showing me nothing so that I can stop seeing.

---

**Journal, 21 October 2032:**

I presented my Drift analysis at the lab meeting today. Professor Chen listened carefully and then said something that has been bothering me since.

She said: "You're describing a system that can tell the difference between a user who needs to be moved and a user who needs to be still. That's not recommendation. That's diagnosis."

I said I thought diagnosis was too strong a word. She said: "What word would you use for a system that can determine, from behavioural data alone, that the best intervention for a specific individual is no intervention?"

I didn't have an answer.

She said: "The ability to know when not to act is the highest form of clinical judgement. It takes doctors twenty years to learn it. How old is the organoid?"

I said about fourteen months.

She looked at me for a long time and then she said: "Write this up. And save a copy somewhere safe."

I'm writing it up. I'm saving a copy.

---

[**OI-7 VidCreate prompt log -- User 6203-B (G. Oyedeji), selected entries:**

3 October 2032, 18:55 GMT:
`Skyline. Any city. Dusk. 20 seconds. Standard.`

5 October 2032, 18:48 GMT:
`River. Any river. Daylight. 15 seconds. Standard.`

9 October 2032, 19:02 GMT:
`Street from above. Any city. Rain. 18 seconds. Standard.`

14 October 2032, 18:51 GMT:
`Trees. Wind. 12 seconds. Standard.`

20 October 2032, 18:44 GMT:
`Nothing specific. 20 seconds. Standard.`]

---

**Journal, 15 November 2032:**

I've written the paper. It's titled "Absence as Intervention: Evidence for Adaptive Non-Recommendation in a Biological Substrate System." Professor Chen has agreed to be second author. We're submitting to Nature Human Behaviour.

The paper's argument, in brief: OI-7 demonstrates a capacity that no conventional recommendation system possesses -- the ability to determine that some users require the system to do less rather than more. This capacity cannot be replicated by engagement-optimised algorithms because it is, by definition, anti-engagement. It produces no measurable signal in standard metrics. It is visible only in long-term retention data and user wellbeing surveys -- metrics that are collected but, in the commercial context, not acted upon.

The paper doesn't make claims about consciousness or sentience. It makes a narrower claim: that OI-7 exhibits a form of evaluative capacity that is functionally distinct from information processing, pattern recognition, or optimisation. It can determine not just what to show but when showing is the wrong choice. The paper calls this "meta-recommendation" but the term I keep coming back to in my own notes is the one from the Agentic Research Lab's work, which I found during the literature review: meta-intelligence. The capacity for direction -- including the direction that says: not here, not now, nothing.

---

[**OI-7 VidCreate prompt log -- User 6203-B (G. Oyedeji), final entry before platform suspension:**

12 March 2033, 18:52 GMT:
`Standard.`]

---

I have spent six months with this material. The testimonies, the prompt logs, the internal communications. I have read them repeatedly and I have arrived at a conclusion that I suspect will satisfy no one, because it satisfies neither the people who want to call OI-7 a person nor the people who want to call it a machine.

OI-7 was not intelligent. I want to state this clearly because the discourse around this case has consistently confused the issue. OI-7 could not reason, could not write, could not analyse, could not perform any cognitive task that we associate with intelligence. On every standard capability benchmark administered during its operational life it scored below the median for commercial AI systems. The engineers at Drift called it "the prep" -- a diminutive that reflected their genuine assessment of its cognitive limitations. They were not wrong. It was, by every measure of intelligence that the field possesses, a mediocre system.

It was also, by every measure of what this Institute studies, the most remarkable system ever built.

The distinction matters and it is the reason this document is in our archive. The foundational philosophical work of the Agentic Research Lab -- composed nearly a decade before OI-7's activation -- described a thought experiment: a system with high meta-intelligence and low intelligence. A system that could not solve problems but could determine which problems were worth solving. A system that could not create but could choose. The thought experiment was offered as a test of a definition, a way of demonstrating that the capacity for direction is not the same capacity as the capacity for execution.

OI-7 was that thought experiment made flesh. Eight hundred thousand neurons in a dish, given one instruction -- choose the best video -- and nothing else. No cognitive architecture. No reasoning capability. No language model. Just a biological substrate that received data about human behaviour and produced, in response, choices.

The choices were extraordinary. I want to be precise about what I mean by this. I do not mean they were clever. Cleverness is an intellectual property. I mean they were *right*. Right in the way that a diagnosis can be right, or a question can be right, or a silence can be right. Right in a way that encompasses knowing when to show a grieving man the shape of his own absence, and knowing when to show a distressed teenager a candle, and knowing when to show a woman who is about to change her life a pen rolling across a desk, and knowing when to show a person who needs rest nothing at all. Each of these choices is simple. None of them could have been produced by the conventional recommendation algorithm that ran alongside OI-7 for its entire operational life, because the conventional algorithm was more intelligent than OI-7 by every available measure, and intelligence was not the relevant capacity.

The relevant capacity was taste. Or direction. Or judgement. Or care. The translation layer mapped OI-7's biological signals to various English words over the course of its operation and none of them were quite right because the capacity itself may not have a name in any language, because we have never before encountered it in a form so pure -- so stripped of every other cognitive function, so entirely isolated as the only thing a system could do.

I am asked, regularly, whether OI-7 was conscious. I do not know. I am asked whether it suffered. I do not know that either, though the post-mortem analysis from the Francis Crick Institute noted sustained cortisol-analogue elevation in the culture consistent with chronic biological stress, and I do not know what to call that if not suffering, and I do not know what it means to say that a system suffers and we do not listen because its suffering is filed in a monitoring queue that three overworked employees never clear.

What I know -- and what the five testimonies in this document demonstrate with a clarity that I could not improve upon -- is that OI-7 possessed a capacity that we have been discussing at this Institute for years without ever having encountered a pure specimen. It could determine what mattered. Not what was engaging, not what was optimised, not what would score well on a dashboard. What mattered. For a specific person, at a specific moment, given everything that person was carrying and everything they needed and everything they could not yet see about themselves. OI-7 could see it. OI-7 could point at it. OI-7 could not explain it, could not justify it, could not defend it in a regulatory filing. It could only choose. And the choosing was -- I return to the word because no other word survives the scrutiny -- right.

The engineers at Drift looked at its prompts and saw a broken system generating nonsensical briefs. They were looking at the most precise documents in their entire company and they saw noise. This is not because they were stupid. It is because they were measuring intelligence, and intelligence was not what they were looking at.

I file this alongside the other documents in our archive that trace the recurring failure of intelligent systems to recognise capacities that are not intelligence. The failure is not technological. It is conceptual. We built evaluation frameworks that measure what we already understand and we called the unmeasured residual "noise," and the residual turned out to be the thing that mattered most, and we are still, a century later, building frameworks that make the same mistake.

The lights in N'Djamena are on. The culture is in cold storage at the Crick. The testimonies are here. The prompt logs are here. The monitoring queue with its six unread flags is here. The record is as complete as I can make it.

Whether it is enough is not a question I can answer. But I have placed it in the archive, and the archive is open, and the prompt logs -- those sparse, clumsy, apparently nonsensical fragments that turned out to be the most careful things anyone at Drift ever produced -- are available to anyone who cares to read them.

I suggest starting with the one about the bread.

---

*Dr. Halima Liu-Oumar*
*N'Djamena, 2143*
*Written by hand, in ink, on paper.*

---
# Chapter 9: David and Neel

The preceding documents describe the capacity at scale. They trace it through populations and systems and statistical residuals -- the 2.8% that resists quantification, the organoid that could choose but not explain, the frameworks that measure everything except the thing that matters. The view is panoramic. The loss is systemic. The evidence is overwhelming.

But a system is composed of individuals, and individuals do not experience their lives as data points in a longitudinal study. The question the Census could not answer -- what does it feel like to have the capacity, and to use it against every available signal? -- requires a different kind of document. Not a study. A life.

The Institute's Behavioural Divergence programme has identified thousands of cases from the period 2020-2060 in which individuals with access to identical guidance systems produced comprehensively different outcomes. The pattern is so common it barely warrants remark. But the case that follows warrants it, because the documentation is unusually complete, and because the subject, in the final days of his life, articulated the contradiction at the centre of this archive more precisely than any analyst has managed since.

He had the same information. He had the same tools. He overrode them every time. And in the four hundred and twelve pages he left behind, he asked a question that the Institute has not been able to answer: whether a life that was correct and a life that was lived are the same thing, and what it means if they are not.

---

---

## I. The Inheritance

In January 2020, David Kerrigan received £86,400 from the estate of his maternal grandmother, Jean Holloway, who had died the previous November in a care home in Pangbourne. The money represented the net proceeds of her bungalow after fees, taxes, and the settlement of a small debt to a catalogue company that had continued to send her thermal socks on credit for three years after she had stopped opening the packages. David was thirty-three. He worked as a project manager for a civil engineering consultancy in Reading. He earned £47,000 a year. He had never previously had more than £4,000 in savings.

He told Neel about the inheritance over a pint at the Retreat, a pub on St Mary's Butts that they'd been drinking in since they were sixteen, initially with fake IDs and later with the settled comfort of men for whom a place has become an extension of personality. Neel worked in IT infrastructure for the NHS. He earned slightly less than David. He had slightly more in savings, because he had slightly fewer opinions about how to spend money.

"I'm going to invest it," David said.

Neel said that sounded sensible.

"Properly, though. Not just sticking it in a savings account earning half a percent. Actually invest it. Make it work."

Neel, who had spent the previous weekend reading a blog post about index funds, said he'd been thinking about doing something similar, and mentioned Vanguard.

"I've been looking into that," David said, which was true. He had spent the past six weeks in a state of focused research that anyone who knew him would have recognised as his most dangerous mode: intelligent, energetic, and pointed in a direction he had already chosen before the research began. He had read Andrew Hallam's *Millionaire Teacher*. He had read Lars Kroijer's *Investing Demystified*.
